  ```
# Old Laptop Rebuild - Prompt 2 VS Code and Two-Laptop Workflow

Run in Codex (gpt-5.6-sol, medium effort) from `/home/anant_gupta` on the old laptop: `codex -m gpt-5.6-sol -c model_reasoning_effort=medium`. This is version 2.

## Revision log
- **v2 (2026-10-04)** rewrites v1 around [[Codebases - Two-Laptop SSH Workflow - Locked Decisions and Postmortem]] (ten decisions, checked against live Tailscale and Microsoft docs) and the live facts in [[Old Laptop Rebuild - Build 1 WSL Findings]].
  - Tailscale SSH is now primary, and keyed OpenSSH is only the fallback.
  - The SSH and Tailscale setup is an ordered runbook with a pass/fail check after each step.
  - The WSL idle-timeout fix moved into [[Old Laptop Rebuild - Prompt 1 WSL]] (Follow-up 1). Prompt 2 now gates its network phase on a passed idle test.
  - Added: retire the Acer's old clones, a superseded-by note on one repo's completion gate, the vault scope line, the worktree dependency settings, key expiry, a tested Tunnels fallback, and Build 1's corrected repo and disk facts.
- **Open flag the locked note did not cover:** if Tailscale never runs on a laptop's Windows side, the Acer's Windows `ssh.exe` (which VS Code Remote-SSH uses) has no route to the tailnet. The prompt makes Phase 1 resolve this with evidence and bring the answer to the checkpoint. The rule in Tailscale's docs is one location per machine, not "never on Windows".

## Prompt

~~~text
You are Codex running in the WSL home directory (/home/anant_gupta) of the old laptop: Dell Latitude 5530, Windows 11 Pro, distro "Ubuntu" (Ubuntu 24.04) on WSL2. This is Build 2 of a laptop rebuild. Build 1 is a separate session. It owns .wslconfig, /etc/wsl.conf, caches, shell parity and the host scripts in D:\WSL\ops. Your layer is the WSL side of VS Code, plus how the same codebases work across this laptop (the Dell) and the Acer. Windows-side VS Code and Windows-host settings belong to later builds. Record what you find there, but do not change it.

# Goal
1. Configure WSL-side VS Code on this laptop to match the Acer's, using current best practice for an AI developer.
2. Build and verify the two-laptop workflow: one canonical checkout per repo on this laptop, reached from the Acer over SSH, with GitHub as backup. Both laptops are always on the same page because there is only one copy of the code, node_modules, .venv and logs. No sync daemon touches code.
Do the work in the order given below. Finish and verify each step before starting the next.

# Read first, in full, in this order
All paths are under /mnt/d/Users/_Anant/10_Areas/Documents/Jarvis/40_Resources/CS/Concepts/New Laptop/ (also reachable through the jarvis MCP). Read the vault AGENTS.md before writing there.
1. Codebases/Codebases - Two-Laptop SSH Workflow - Locked Decisions and Postmortem. It is the source of truth. Where it disagrees with this prompt, it wins, unless the live machine disproves it.
2. Old Laptop Rebuild/Old Laptop Rebuild - Build 1 WSL Findings, and the Index in the same folder (live facts and what Build 1 changed).
3. Codebases/wsl-home/VS Code - WSL, Codebases/windows-home/VS Code - Windows, VS Code Professional Setup, VS Code - Install Loop, VS Code - Terminal Environments, VS Code - MCP and Secrets.
4. Both repo directive folders (Codebases/second-brain-claudekit, Codebases/internship-research-loop), and Sync/Cross-Laptop Sync - Known Failure Modes and Prevention.
Notes can be stale. The vault's mirrored CLAUDE.md copies for the two repos are known to be stale, so read each repo's live CLAUDE.md and AGENTS.md in the WSL checkout instead. The live machine wins, and each contradiction goes in your log.

# Verified facts (2026-10-04, from Build 1's audit and my checks)
VS Code and Settings Sync:
- Settings Sync is already active on this laptop. The Acer's cloud copy has landed: the Windows user settings.json here is the Acer's file, and the Windows side has the Acer's 40 extensions. Anything written to a Windows user-level VS Code file here (settings.json, keybindings.json, tasks.json, mcp.json, snippets, extensions) syncs to the Acer within minutes. That is how the 2026-09-25 settings-wipe incident happened.
- The synced file holds Acer-only values: todo-tree.ripgrep.ripgrep points to C:\Users\anant\..., python-envs.pythonProjects points to D:\_Anant\... (this laptop uses C:\Users\Anant Gupta and D:\Users\_Anant), and python.defaultInterpreterPath is the Windows .venv/Scripts/python.exe, which also reaches WSL windows.
- A pre-sync copy of this laptop's settings is at %APPDATA%\Code\User\settings.json.f51dd950.bak. Read it to learn what this laptop lost.
- The Windows VS Code app is at C:\Users\Anant Gupta\AppData\Local\Programs\Microsoft VS Code. The Acer's is on D:.
WSL side:
- VS Code Server is 1.140.0. There are 25 WSL extensions, many stale against the Acer's set (the cpptools family, cmake-tools, mikestead.dotenv, npm-intellisense, ms-azuretools.vscode-docker, es7-react-js-snippets, ms-vscode.powershell).
- ~/.vscode-server/data/Machine/settings.json, ~/.config/vscode-env, ~/.config/mcp and ~/.vscode do not exist. ~/.bashrc had only the nvm lines when I checked. Build 1 adds the shell parity block, so re-check before editing.
- Global git config had no pull.rebase, push.autoSetupRemote, fetch.prune, rerere.enabled, core.autocrlf or init.defaultBranch.
- Claude Code has 2 deny rules. ~/.claude.json has only graphify and pencil at user scope. jarvis, the-plan, jarvis-fs and github are in ~/.mcp.json. WSLENV is empty.
- Build 1 found 58 Git repositories under ~/projects, including nested sandbox and tool repos. Repo state differs from the older notes: second-brain-claudekit is 0 ahead and 0 behind with one modified hook file, and internship-research-loop is 5 ahead and 5 behind with many deletions and untracked docs. gh auth is valid for gupta-builds over HTTPS.
- Editor server folders, by size: .vscode-server 7.74 GiB (cached VSIX alone 1.75), .cursor-server 3.57 GiB (snapshots 1.14), .vscode-remote-containers 1.03 GiB (nine old hashes). Build 1 deferred these to you.
- A hidden Windows scheduled task, ConversationCapture-Backfill-WSL, starts WSL every 30 minutes. It belongs to the Windows-host build.
- wslview is broken because this boot registered WSLInterop-late. Build 1 owns that repair.
Two-laptop:
- Tailscale and an SSH server are not installed. The Windows OpenSSH server is not running. The Remote-SSH extension is installed on the Windows side.
- This laptop is the desk machine (monitor, keyboard, mouse), on the Ultimate Performance power plan with AC sleep set to never. The Acer stands alone, with a discrete NVIDIA GPU. This laptop has none.
- networkingMode=mirrored, and Jarvis MCP depends on it. Build 1 is adding vmIdleTimeout=-1 under [wsl2] and instanceIdleTimeout=-1 under [general] to .wslconfig, then running the idle test.
- Today each laptop has its own clones and they meet only through GitHub, with <machine>/<topic> branches (dell-latitude/..., acer-predator/...). The single-host design replaces that model.
- You cannot type a sudo password. Put every sudo step in a script file for me to run. Never run wsl --shutdown. Never edit .wslconfig or /etc/wsl.conf, because Build 1 owns them. Tailscale login and the Tailscale admin console are mine to do. Give me exact click paths.

# Locked rules (carry these into everything you build)
R1. This laptop is the canonical host. Every repo that both laptops touch has exactly one checkout, on this laptop's WSL ext4 filesystem under ~/projects. Never work from /mnt/c or /mnt/d. GitHub is the backup and the PR channel.
R2. Tailscale runs inside this WSL distro only. It is never installed on this laptop's Windows side. Running it on both the Windows host and inside WSL on the same machine breaks WSL's outbound traffic (Tailscale's WSL2 install doc, double encapsulation).
R3. Tailscale SSH is the primary SSH mechanism (tailscale up --ssh, with node identity instead of keys). Keyed OpenSSH is the fallback, and its hardening file (PasswordAuthentication no, KbdInteractiveAuthentication no, PermitRootLogin no, AllowUsers anant_gupta, AllowTcpForwarding yes) only matters on that path. Tailscale SSH takes over tailnet port 22, so make the fallback sshd listen on a different port (2222) and check how Ubuntu 24.04's ssh.socket affects a port change. Accept explicitly that Tailscale SSH does not check a client key pair, so any OS user on a tailnet device can connect as the configured WSL user.
R4. The host must stay reachable. That needs both idle keys from Build 1 plus the idle test passing. An active sshd service alone does not keep the distro alive. Phase 4 does not start until the idle test has passed.
R5. Parallel tasks use git worktrees, with one VS Code window per worktree. One session owns a branch at a time. Branches are <machine>/<topic>.
R6. Reproducibility lives in git, not in a sync tool: lockfiles, .nvmrc or .node-version, the packageManager field with corepack, .python-version and uv.lock, .vscode/extensions.json, .editorconfig. node_modules, .venv, logs, caches and build output are never shared or synced.
R7. Scope is ~/projects only. The Jarvis and The Plan vaults keep their Syncthing and git-auto-sync mechanism unchanged. Do not touch .stignore, .gitignore, or either vault's scheduled tasks.
R8. Key expiry: the dell-wsl node must have key expiry disabled in the Tailscale admin console, or Remote-SSH silently stops working after the default expiry. This is a one-time step for me.
R9. Fallback when this laptop is off: the Acer clones from GitHub, pushes a <machine>/<topic> branch, and this laptop fetches later. Last-resort fallback is VS Code Tunnels, which only works while VS Code or the `code tunnel` CLI is running on the host, so test whether a persistent service (`code tunnel service install` or equivalent) works before it is ever needed.
R10. This is not a devcontainer and not a portable environment definition. Do not add one.

# Questions Phase 1 must answer with evidence, not assumption
Q1. How does the Acer reach the tailnet? VS Code Remote-SSH on the Acer uses the Windows ssh.exe, which has no tailnet route if Tailscale never runs on the Acer's Windows side. Test or document the options and give me a recommendation: (a) a ProxyCommand through the Acer's WSL Tailscale (for example `wsl.exe ... tailscale nc %h %p`), (b) a remote.SSH.path wrapper, (c) Tailscale on the Acer's Windows side only, never in the Acer's WSL (one location per machine, which Tailscale's docs allow). Say which one you recommend and why. This changes R2's wording for the Acer, so I decide at the checkpoint.
Q2. Does Tailscale work correctly inside WSL under networkingMode=mirrored? Run a live round-trip test, not just `tailscale status`. After `tailscale up`, confirm the MTU adjustment from 1280 to 1340 that Tailscale's docs describe (`ip link show`).
Q3. On this personal tailnet, does Tailscale SSH work with the default access policy, or does it need an explicit ssh block? Sources disagree. Check the admin console's Access Controls page with me and report. Do not assert either answer.
Q4. Does pnpm's global virtual store setting exist under the name in the Locked Decisions note, for the installed pnpm version? Check the pnpm docs for the exact setting name. Also check which repos actually use pnpm: the clone notes say second-brain-claudekit has no root package.json, so confirm before proposing anything there.
Q5. Do uv's cache directory and the path of each uv project resolve to the same filesystem? Compare device IDs with `df`.
Q6. What does the current Codex IDE extension use to run Codex in WSL, and does the Acer-over-SSH case change anything for it?

# Target state, WSL-side VS Code
1. Remote settings file ~/.vscode-server/data/Machine/settings.json with the Acer's values from VS Code - WSL: bash as the default terminal, Python Environments activation, python.condaPath, an explicit Linux python.defaultInterpreterPath that overrides the synced Windows one, Jupyter hiding system and conda-base interpreters from the kernel picker, and watcher excludes (.venv, node_modules, target). Machine-scoped keys (chat.agent.sandbox.*, claudeCode machine-scope settings) belong here. Leave the agent sandbox off. ## Claude
  ```

This is a design question, not a prompt edit, so I'll answer it before touching any prompt. I'm checking Tailscale's own WSL documentation first, because the answer depends on exactly what it says about WSL, Windows and SSH host keys.

Short answer: **Linux/WSL on the Dell, and probably Windows on the Acer.** Tailscale's own docs don't support a WSL-only rule for both machines. They also show that Windows-side Tailscale doesn't undercut your WSL plan.

## What Tailscale actually is
Tailscale is network plumbing. It gives a machine, or an environment inside it, an address on your private network. It doesn't hold code, toolchains or agents, so installing it on Windows moves nothing out of WSL. The choice depends on one question per machine: **what has to be reachable, and what only has to reach out?**

## The Dell (the host): WSL, and it has no real alternative
- Tailscale SSH is our primary mechanism, and its server component runs only on Linux and macOS. Windows isn't supported as a server. The thing being SSH'd into is the WSL distro, where the code lives, so Tailscale and `sshd` belong there.
- The docs call a WSL node "an advanced concept", but it's the supported way to give WSL its own identity, and this is the case that needs it. The `dell-wsl` node stays.

## The Acer (client only): where the choice is real
Nothing needs to reach into the Acer. It only needs to reach the Dell, and the program doing that is Windows VS Code, which uses `ssh.exe` on the Windows side.

- **Tailscale's own recommendation:** the WSL2 page says, quoting it, "it is recommended that users run Tailscale on the Windows host only, and not inside WSL 2." Its hard warning is only about running both on the same machine, because the packets get wrapped twice and stop working.
- **WSL-only on the Acer has no documented path for VS Code.** Tailscale's pages never mention VS Code or Remote-SSH, and `tailscale nc` isn't documented as an SSH `ProxyCommand`. A wrapper chain like `ssh.exe → wsl.exe → tailscale nc` might work, but it's untested. It also depends on the Acer's WSL being awake. The one-time host-key prompt would appear too, because Tailscale's host-key sync isn't documented for Windows `ssh.exe`.

That makes "no Tailscale on Windows" the more fragile choice for the Acer.

## Your concern
"If it is on Windows then we have no point of WSL on the new laptop." I'd push back on that one. Windows-side Tailscale on the Acer installs no dev tooling on Windows. The Acer's WSL still hosts its toolchains, the discrete GPU work and its own repos. It also reaches the Dell through the Windows route, so terminal sessions work too.

## Options on the Acer
1. **Tailscale on Windows only, never inside the Acer's WSL (my recommendation).** It's Tailscale's recommended setup. Remote-SSH works with a plain `ssh dell-wsl` and no wrappers. The "one location per machine" rule is still satisfied.
2. **Tailscale in WSL only, as you want.** The Acer would work through its WSL terminal: `ssh dell-wsl`, tmux, and the `claude`/`codex` CLIs running on the Dell. VS Code on the Acer would use Remote Tunnels instead of Remote-SSH, with no Tailscale on that path. The cost is a dependency on Microsoft's relay and a GitHub sign-in. A `ProxyCommand` route would stay untested until Codex proves it live.

I haven't changed the prompt. Which option do you want? I'd write it into Prompt 2 as a locked rule, replacing Q1's open question, and update the Locked Decisions note's Decision 1 so it matches the docs.

**Tool calls:**

I need: (a) the exact warning about running Tailscale on both the Windows host and inside WSL2 at the same time; (b) what it says about reaching the tailnet from inside WSL when Tailscale runs ONLY on the Windows host; (c) what it recommends for making a WSL2 instance reachable (own node); (d) anything about mirrored vs NAT networking mode.\n2. https://tailscale.com/docs/features/tailscale-ssh : which OSes can be an SSH SERVER (is Windows supported as a server?), which can be a CLIENT, whether a plain OpenSSH client (including Windows ssh.exe) can connect to a Tailscale SSH server, how SSH host keys are distributed to clients (does it write to the client's system known_hosts, and on which OSes?), and the ACL 'ssh' block requirement for personal tailnets.\n3. https://tailscale.com/docs/reference/tailscale-cli (or the 'tailscale nc' section): what `tailscale nc` does, and whether it is documented as an SSH ProxyCommand and when.\n4. Search tailscale.com docs for guidance on using VS Code Remote-SSH with Tailscale, and for any statement about Windows clients connecting to a Linux/WSL Tailscale SSH server from a machine where Tailscale runs only inside WSL.\nReport only what the pages say; flag anything you could not find. Do not speculate."}`

We also establish the base for d drive correctly, removing a lot of things, correctly coinfiguring wsl in the first prompt itself (takes care .wslconfig, etc. files needed to be set from windows exclusively). To summarize, you are required to write two set of prompts over for the windows build. Write them in one single note so it is easy to copy and paste into a new session. This session is going to working inside the c drive home directory so will have access to read through literally everything that the wiztree is showing in detail. Something to note is that the wiztree images are only provided to the session which is running prompt 1. The prompt 2 will nothave wiztree images.

## Claude

Before writing anything I'll read the Sonnet 5.5 prompting guide, then dig through the Jarvis notes on terminal, Windows and Google Drive setup, and take read-only readings of this laptop's Windows side.

I'm reading the rest of this laptop's Windows snapshot, then drafting the two prompts.

The gate facts are the ones stated just above: links, one section affected, no data files, and your instruction quoted. Retrying the edit.

Both Windows prompts are in one note, and I haven't run either one. The note is `Old Laptop Rebuild - Windows Prompts (Build 3 C Drive Cleanup and Build 4 Terminal Parity).md` in the Old Laptop Rebuild folder, and each prompt sits in its own fenced block for copy-paste. I also renumbered the Index session list to match.

**Which prompt is which.** You described the terminal prompt first but said the WizTree images go to prompt 1, and that the first prompt also handles `.wslconfig` and the D: baseline. So I read it as cleanup first, terminal second:
- **Build 3 is the C: cleanup.** It covers the Google Drive mirror, the D: baseline and the Windows-side WSL host settings. The WizTree screenshots go in the same message as this prompt.
- **Build 4 is the terminal installs and config parity.** It gets no images.

Say if you meant the reverse.

**How to run them.** Both go in Claude Code on the Dell, started from `C:\Users\Anant Gupta`, with Sonnet 5.5 picked via `/model`. Build 3 runs at high effort and Build 4 at medium. The sessions run unelevated and write each admin step to a script under `D:\WSL\ops\windows\` for you to run.

**Build 3 (cleanup):**
- It transcribes the screenshots, crops and zooms them where numbers are hard to read, and checks every decision-driving figure against live scans.
- It then investigates the real crash causes and why the D: pagefile never gets created, and stops at a checkpoint with a plan. Deletions come in three tiers. You approve Tier A (regenerable data) once with "go A", Tier B (apps, user data) per item, and Tier C (system, vaults) is never touched.
- A sensitive-data section keeps secrets and personal documents out of Google Drive, logs and notes. Findings list them by path and category only.
- Google Drive uses Mirror mode with the mirror folder on D:. The prompt tells it that the Jarvis and The Plan vaults must stay out, since Syncthing handles them.

**Build 4 (terminal):**
- It installs PowerShell 7, the Iosevka Nerd Font, Starship, git with delta, and ripgrep, fd, bat, fzf, zoxide, jq, lazygit, Yazi and uv, using winget only.
- It writes the shared config files byte-identical, as UTF-8 without a BOM and with LF line endings. They are hash-checked across the Acer snapshot, Windows and WSL. Plain `Set-Content` would break that by adding CRLF.
- It points each tool at the shared config folder with user environment variables.
- It produces a parity matrix and startup timings.

**Things I found that you should know:**
- **The Dell has almost none of the terminal toolkit.** It has no PowerShell 7, Starship, Yazi or Nerd Font, only Windows PowerShell 5.1 and Cascadia fonts.
- **Fixing the font heals VS Code.** The synced VS Code settings already name `IosevkaTerm NFM`, so installing the font restores terminal icons without touching any VS Code file.
- **Two odd files in your profile.** `0K7YY.exe` and `nc6a9.exe` could be old installers or something worse, so the prompt makes the session check each one's signature and hash before you decide. A file named `NUL` also needs a special path to delete.
- **Three Google accounts have signed in to Drive on this Dell.** The prompt says to identify them by metadata only and not to sign in or out without you.

**Appendix prompt for the Acer.** The Acer's real config files aren't reachable from here, and the Dell's WSL configs were rebuilt from notes. For "exactly the same", I added a small appendix prompt to run on the Acer first. It exports only non-secret terminal configs and tool versions into a vault folder, with a hash manifest, and Syncthing carries it to the Dell. Without it, Build 4 rebuilds the configs from notes and labels them that way.

**Not covered here.** Windows VS Code user files, the MCP registry and Claude Code settings are left for a later build. Anything written to those on the Dell reaches the Acer through Settings Sync.

Send me each session's output when it finishes, and I'll write the follow-ups into this note.

  ```
# Google Drive Sync Policy

## Why this note exists

The user believed this policy was already written down somewhere in this folder — verified 2026-09-13 that it was not. This note is the first real record of it. It governs Google Drive's desktop "back up folders from your computer" feature specifically, not the still-undecided cross-laptop sync tool (Syncthing/Tailscale, see [[New Laptop Setup#Cross-laptop scope]]) — those are separate systems.

## The rule

Sync an original, non-regenerable piece of content. Never sync anything a tool can rebuild from something else (git history, a package manager, a compiler).

**Sync:** documents, PDFs, slides, forms, spreadsheets, notes, write-ups, exported reports, photos, videos, financial records, applications.

**Never sync:** `.git`, `node_modules`, `.venv`/`venv`, `__pycache__`, `dist`, `build`, `bin`, `obj`, `target`, `.dll`/`.pdb`/`.exe` build output, `.claude`/`.codex`/`.cursor` tool config/cache, Docker build contexts/images, `.idea`/`.vs` IDE metadata.

## Why this matters beyond clutter

[[Ubuntu - WSL#Contrast / What It Is Not]] already establishes the technical reason independently: WSL's entire filesystem lives inside one opaque `.vhdx` file — Drive/OneDrive can only back up files they can see inside, so nothing WSL-side was ever syncable at the file level anyway. This policy generalizes that same boundary to Windows-side code too.

## Concrete evidence this was a real, live problem (2026-09-13)

A direct Drive search for "Backup" during this session's cleanup surfaced individual raw `.py` files actively uploading from the "UMN" backed-up folder, owned by the UMN university account (`gupt0479@umn.edu`): `BackupRead_BackupWrite.py`, `BackupEventLog.py`, `BackupSeek_streamheaders.py` — loose coursework source files, not documents. This is what "too much being synced, including the .git folder" looked like in practice.

## Applied to the current 7-folder Drive Desktop backup list

| Folder | Verdict |
|---|---|
| `00_Inbox`, `20_Progress`, `30_Resources`, `Finance`, `Job & Apartment` | Keep as-is — pure documents |
| `Real coding` (1.3GB) | Remove from the backup list entirely. GitHub is its real backup. Extract any standalone write-ups/PDFs first if worth keeping. |
| `UMN` (3.3GB) | Split: create a `UMN-Documents` folder with only syllabi/slides/PDFs/notes, sync that instead. Anything that's a real repo (`.git`, Dockerfile, build output, an assignment like the Backup*.py files above) stays out of Drive. |

## Known tool limitation driving the "split, don't configure" approach

Google Drive for desktop cannot exclude a subfolder by pattern inside a folder already selected for backup (confirmed 2026-09-13, still true) — selective sync only works at the top level. There is no `.gitignore`-equivalent inside the app. The fix has to be structural: physically separate document-only content into its own top-level folder before adding it to the backup list, not a settings toggle. Source: [Google Drive Community](https://support.google.com/drive/thread/13418864?hl=en).

## Account-lifespan risks this policy also has to account for

- **UMN Google Workspace account** — access ends at graduation/affiliation end. Treat it as disposable; nothing meant to last belongs there permanently, only currently-relevant coursework documents.
- **Google One subscription on `anantmahi721@gmail.com` is a 1-year term.** Don't treat the current generous storage as permanent. GitHub (code) and the local D: drive stay the actual source of truth; Drive is a convenience copy, not the archive of record.

## Open item

The "Entire Move" → "Laptop Backup (45GB)" shared shortcut's actual target folder could not be resolved via the Drive API from this session (tried three search approaches, 2026-09-13). Needs the user to paste the real folder URL or move it into My Drive directly before its contents can be reviewed.
  ```

  ```
# C Drive Bloat - Failure Log and Prevention Rules

## One-Line Answer
==Moving the WSL disk to D: protected C: from almost nothing: the Dell's C: is 93% used because dozens of small defaults (tool caches, Electron apps, a fallback pagefile, old redirects never cleaned up) each landed on C: separately, and Build 1's 41.7 GiB reclaim happened entirely on D: and inside ext4.==

## Measured state (Dell, read-only scan 2026-10-07)
| Item | Value | Source |
|---|---:|---|
| C: size / used / free | 252 GB / 93% / about 20 GiB | `df /mnt/c` |
| C: free, 2026-10-04 start of Build 1 | 28.65 GiB | Build 1 findings |
| C: free, after Build 1 | 26.30 GiB | Build 1 findings |
| C: free, 2026-10-07 | about 20 GiB | live; the drop is about 6 GiB in three days |
| `C:\pagefile.sys` | 31.49 GiB (was 25.6 on 10-04) | live; peak use only 6.3 GiB, current 4.6 GiB |
| Profile, non-AppData | about 29 GiB | scan |
| `AppData\Local` top | Programs 14.1, Spotify 6.38, Vivaldi 5.63, Microsoft 4.87, superwhisper 4.82, **npm-cache 4.31**, Packages 3.68, Temp 3.37, **uv 2.51**, hermes 2.09, WisprFlow 1.88, **ms-playwright 1.34** (GiB) | scan |
| Profile folders | miniconda3 7.09, **vscode-remote-wsl 6.29**, .vscode 4.08, .codex 3.36, **[REDACTED] 2.34**, .cache 1.44, .local 1.18, .kiro 0.91 (GiB) | scan |
| Roaming and Windows folders | not measured in this pass. 10-04 WizTree: Roaming Claude 10.7, Code 3.4, Jan 3.3, Cursor 2.8, Kiro 2.6, npm 1.8 | [[Old Laptop Rebuild - Index]] |

## Failure log
| # | What went wrong | Evidence | Root cause | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Pagefile on C: is 31.5 GiB although Windows is configured for `d:\pagefile.sys` | `Win32_PageFileSetting`: `d:\pagefile.sys`, size 0/0, `AutomaticManagedPagefile` False. `Win32_PageFileUsage`: only `C:\pagefile.sys`, 32,247 MB allocated, peak 6,471 MB | The configured D: file does not get created, so Windows falls back to a RAM-sized file on C:. Why D: fails is a hypothesis; confirm in the event log before changing anything | Open, Windows-host build |
| 2 | npm cache redirect "missed" | `npm config get cache` now returns `D:\npm-cache`, but `Local\npm-cache` still holds 4.31 GiB | The redirect was set later and the old cache was never removed. It is a stale copy, not live data | Open: verify the D: cache, then delete the C: copy |
| 3 | uv, Playwright, pip caches default to C: | `UV_CACHE_DIR`, `PIP_CACHE_DIR`, `PLAYWRIGHT_BROWSERS_PATH` unset for the user; `Local\uv` 2.51 GiB, `ms-playwright` 1.34 GiB | Each tool needs its own env var; the D: policy was written per tool, never as a rule | Open |
| 4 | WSL swap lands on C: | Acer notes; the Dell had no `swapfile=` | `swapfile` defaults to `%TEMP%\swap.vhdx` | Fixed on the Dell by Build 1 (`D:\WSL\swap.vhdx`, 8 GiB) |
| 5 | Moving the VHDX to D: was read as "WSL is off C:" | Index facts; 9.26 GiB of WSL-adjacent files sit on C: | `vscode-remote-wsl` (6.29), the old Codex quarantine (2.34) and `%TEMP%\wsl-crashes` (0.54) are outside the VHDX | Open, Windows-host build |
| 6 | Electron and Store apps are stuck on C: | [[Installations]] already says so; Spotify 6.38, Vivaldi 5.63, superwhisper 4.82, WisprFlow 1.88 | Their installers offer no location choice | Accepted. Budget for them; clear their caches |
| 7 | Windows-side editors and agents grow on C: | `.vscode` 4.08, `.codex` 3.36, Local\Programs 14.1 (VS Code installed on C: here, on D: on the Acer) | Install location and extension cache are not redirectable per tool | Open |
| 8 | A cleanup created more C: data | `[REDACTED]` 2.34 GiB | "Quarantine" moved files to the same drive and nothing reviewed them | Open: decide, then delete |
| 9 | Free space was reported from the wrong place | Build 1's 41.7 GiB reclaim changed D: and ext4 only; C: free fell | Different drives; host reclaim also needs trim plus compaction | Rule below |

## Growth rules (apply to both laptops)
| Source | Standing rule | One check command (PowerShell) |
|---|---|---|
| Free-space floor | Treat C: under 60 GB free as "act now" and under 30 GB as an incident. The statusline warning at 30 GB is too late for a 250 GB drive | `Get-PSDrive C \| % { [math]::Round($_.Free/1GB,1) }` |
| Pagefile | Explicit size on a drive that works, with a measured cap (peak use plus headroom), never a RAM-sized fallback. The Windows-host build chooses the numbers | `Get-CimInstance Win32_PageFileUsage \| select Name,AllocatedBaseSize,PeakUsage` |
| Tool caches | Set the env var at user scope the day the tool is installed: `npm config set cache`, `UV_CACHE_DIR`, `PIP_CACHE_DIR`, `PLAYWRIGHT_BROWSERS_PATH`, `OLLAMA_MODELS`. After any redirect, delete the old C: copy in the same step | `npm config get cache; [Environment]::GetEnvironmentVariable('UV_CACHE_DIR','User')` |
| WSL files | VHDX, swap and Docker data on D:. Review `vscode-remote-wsl`, `%TEMP%` and any dated quarantine monthly | `Get-ChildItem $env:USERPROFILE -Force \| ? Name -match 'wsl\|quarantine'` |
| Installers | Before installing, check whether the installer has a location option. If not, list it as "stuck on C:" in [[Installations]] with its expected size | Compare `Local\Programs` and `Program Files` against [[Installations]] |

The Acer's C: is 315 GB with D: at 637 GB, so the same classes of growth have more room, not no growth. From the vault only: npm cache is on `D:\npm-cache`, known folders are on D:, and Google Drive's DriveFS cache is still on C: with no supported redirect ([[Acer Live State — 2026-09-16]]). Everything in this section is from the vault and has not been measured on the Acer.

# Old Laptop Rebuild - Index

## One-Line Answer
==The old Dell (Latitude 5530) gets rebuilt to match the Acer in sessions run one layer at a time, WSL first, and every session logs what it measured and the rule that stops the same growth from returning.==

## Status
Build 1 WSL Phase 3 executed 2026-10-04 ([[Old Laptop Rebuild - Build 1 WSL Findings]]): 36.424 GiB of verified project artifacts plus 5.304 GiB of native caches reclaimed inside ext4; user-space parity/config applied; `.wslconfig` backed up and aligned for the always-on SSH host.
Update 2026-10-07: WSL 3.0.1.0 is live. Resource/swap verification passed (19 GiB, 10 processors, 8 GiB on `D:\WSL\swap.vhdx`), `wslview` and both mirrored Obsidian endpoints pass. The safe sparse refusal should have been SKIPPED, not fatal; the original idle FAIL was a PowerShell 5.1 decoding bug. Corrected ASCII scripts are under `D:\WSL\ops`, with dated backups. The post-update verifier measured 0 `CheckConnection` events in 120 seconds. The corrected idle test passed: Ubuntu remained Running after 75 seconds with no WSL call during the wait. Systemd is still `degraded` because `systemd-binfmt.service` failed; its only configured Python registration exactly matches one already live, suggesting a duplicate registration, but the unit log rotated away so this remains a hypothesis. Build 2 Phase 2 items 1-6 are now applied and verified; see [[Old Laptop Rebuild - Build 2 VS Code and Two-Laptop Findings]]. Phase 3 checkpoint is ready. Host health is not fully clean until the systemd caveat is understood or accepted. C: has 20.08 GiB free and a 32,247 MB pagefile (6,471 MB peak); D: has 363.80 GiB free. See [[C Drive Bloat - Failure Log and Prevention Rules]].

## Two-laptop workflow (locked direction, 2026-10-04)
The Dell is the canonical host: one checkout per shared repo on its WSL ext4 disk. The Acer connects through VS Code Remote-SSH over Tailscale. Tailscale runs in exactly one place per machine: inside WSL on the Dell (the Tailscale SSH server is Linux/macOS only), and on the Windows side only on the Acer (Remote-SSH uses the Windows `ssh.exe`). Decided 2026-10-04. GitHub stays the backup and PR channel, parallel tasks use git worktrees with `<machine>/<topic>` branches, and no sync daemon touches code. Cost: the Dell must be awake and on the tailnet. Fallback when it is off: the Acer pushes a branch from its own clone. Reasoning: a single copy cannot drift, which is the property the Jarvis sync could not give without constant repair ([[Cross-Laptop Sync - Known Failure Modes and Prevention]]). Network exposure (Tailscale, sshd) needs the user's approval inside the session.

## Settings Sync is live on the Dell
The Acer's cloud copy has landed on the Dell's Windows VS Code, with Acer-only paths in the synced `settings.json`. Anything written to a Windows user-level VS Code file on the Dell reaches the Acer. Build 2 stays out of those files and records findings for the Windows-side build.

|---|---|

| Disk | One Samsung PM9A1 1 TB NVMe. C: 251.9 GB (28.6 GB free), D: 700 GB (about 366 GB free) |
| C: biggest | Users 128 GB (AppData 100 GB: Local 67.8, Roaming 32.2), Windows 39 GB, pagefile.sys 25.6 GB, Program Files 26 GB |

| Local heavy | Programs 15 GB, Spotify 6.2, Vivaldi 5.8, Microsoft 5.0, superwhisper 4.9, npm-cache 4.5 (redirect to D: missed), Packages 3.8, Temp 3.6, hermes 2.3, WisprFlow 1.9, ms-playwright 1.4. `Local/Google` reports a bogus size (Drive virtual files), so skip it when measuring |

## Findings that change the plan
- **There is no discrete GPU.** "Use the GPU" means the Iris Xe only. CUDA, NVIDIA container tooling and GPU-passthrough tuning do not apply. Heavy local-model work belongs on the Acer.
- **The pagefile warning has a visible cause.** Windows reports `D:\pagefile.sys` as the configured pagefile (system-managed, `AutomaticManagedPagefile` off), but no pagefile exists on D:. The live one is a 25.6 GB `C:\pagefile.sys`. The likely story is that Windows cannot create the D: file at boot, falls back to a temporary C: file, and shows the "pagefile" pop-up. This is a hypothesis for the Windows-host session to confirm in the event log. The fix is an explicit, sized pagefile configuration, not another reboot.
- **WSL swap defaults to C:.** No `swapfile=` setting exists, the same bug the Acer notes record.
- **Jarvis MCP needs mirrored networking.** From WSL, `127.0.0.1:27123` is Obsidian on Windows only in mirrored mode ([[VS Code - MCP and Secrets]]). The statement in [[Ubuntu - WSL]] that NAT alone forwards localhost both ways is only true from Windows to WSL, so the Dell keeps mirrored.
- **A past quarantine sits on C:.** `[REDACTED]` (2.3 GB) is on the drive being freed.
- **Running `wsl --shutdown` from inside a WSL session kills the session.** Host steps go in a script the user runs.

## Session sequence
1. WSL layer ([[Old Laptop Rebuild - Prompt 1 WSL]]): audit, `.wslconfig`, tool parity, WSL-side cleanup, Jarvis log.
2. Windows host, `C:\Users\Anant Gupta`: pagefile, AppData cleanup (Roaming/Claude, Local), Temp and crash dumps, quarantine folder, startup and services, Defender exclusions for the vhdx and `~/projects`, power plan.
3. D: offload: `wsl-ubuntu.tar`, game archives, recycle-bin Rust target dirs, Docker prune, model files.
4. Terminal parity (Windows Terminal, PowerShell profile, Starship).
5. VS Code parity (extensions 39/34, settings, MCP registry).
6. Dev-only profile and a final verification pass against the Acer.

## Logging rule for every session
Each session writes `Old Laptop Rebuild - Build N <layer> Findings.md` here, with a before/after size table, every state-changing command, the root cause of every error, and a Growth rules section (the standing rule and one check command per thing that grew).
  ```

  ```
40:4. **Chris Titus Tech / Windows Toolbox** is a WinGet/Chocolatey batch-install and Windows-tweak wrapper. 189:Why can't Discord/Slack/Zoom/Telegram/WhatsApp/Claude Desktop be installed to D: even with Chris Titus Tech or winget?::Their installers (Squirrel-based Electron, or Microsoft Store) expose no location option — a technical constraint of the installer, not a policy choice.
195:Does a silent/unattended winget install still show an installer's location picker if one exists?::Generally no — silent mode skips the prompt and takes the installer's own default (usually C:), even for installers that would ask interactively. Apps with a real location choice need an explicit `winget install --location` override or a manual GUI run to land on D:.
  ```

  ```
$ErrorActionPreference = 'SilentlyContinue'
function H($t) { "`n=== $t ===" }
H 'OS / PowerShell'
(Get-CimInstance Win32_OperatingSystem | Select-Object Caption,BuildNumber | Format-List | Out-String).Trim()
"PS: $($PSVersionTable.PSVersion)  pwsh7: $((Get-Command pwsh).Source)"
H 'Profile paths'
"CurrentUserAllHosts: $($PROFILE.CurrentUserAllHosts)"
"CurrentUserCurrentHost: $($PROFILE.CurrentUserCurrentHost)  exists=$(Test-Path $PROFILE.CurrentUserCurrentHost)"
"MyDocuments: $([Environment]::GetFolderPath('MyDocuments'))"
"Desktop: $([Environment]::GetFolderPath('Desktop'))"
"Downloads shell folder: $((Get-ItemProperty 'HKCU:\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Explorer\User Shell Folders').'{[REDACTED]}')"
H 'Tools on PATH'
foreach ($c in 'wt','wezterm','pwsh','git','gh','starship','rg','fd','bat','fzf','zoxide','delta','lazygit','yazi','atuin','jq','tmux','node','npm','pnpm','uv','python','conda','rustup','cargo','docker','code','claude','codex','kiro-cli','agy','winget','scoop','choco','tailscale','ssh','7z','nanazip','ncdu','win32yank') {
  $p = (Get-Command $c -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue | Select-Object -First 1).Source
  "{0,-10} {1}" -f $c, $(if ($p) { $p } else { '-' })
}
H 'Fonts matching Iosevka / Nerd'
Add-Type -AssemblyName System.Drawing
(New-Object System.Drawing.Text.InstalledFontCollection).Families | Where-Object { $_.Name -match 'Iosevka|Nerd|Cascadia|JetBrains|FiraCode|Hack' } | ForEach-Object { $_.Name }
H 'Config dirs in home'
Get-ChildItem $env:USERPROFILE -Force -Directory | Where-Object { $_.Name -match '^\.(config|local|cache|wezterm|ssh|gitconfig)|wezterm|Scripts$' } | ForEach-Object { $_.FullName }
Get-ChildItem "$env:USERPROFILE\.config" -Force | ForEach-Object { "  .config\$($_.Name)" }
Get-ChildItem $env:USERPROFILE -Force -File | Where-Object { $_.Name -match 'wezterm|starship|\.gitconfig|\.condarc|\.wslconfig|\.npmrc' } | ForEach-Object { $_.FullName }
H 'Windows Terminal settings'
$wt = Get-ChildItem "$env:LOCALAPPDATA\Packages" -Directory | Where-Object Name -like 'Microsoft.WindowsTerminal*' | Select-Object -First 1
if ($wt) { "WT package: $($wt.Name)"; "settings.json exists: $(Test-Path (Join-Path $wt.FullName 'LocalState\settings.json'))" } else { 'Windows Terminal package not found' }
H 'WSL config files'
Get-Content "$env:USERPROFILE\.wslconfig"
wsl --version 2>&1 | ForEach-Object { $_ -replace "`0", '' }
H 'Env (names + non-secret values)'
foreach ($n in 'UV_CACHE_DIR','PIP_CACHE_DIR','PLAYWRIGHT_BROWSERS_PATH','OLLAMA_MODELS','npm_config_cache','CONDA_PKGS_DIRS','WSLENV','TEMP','TMP') { "{0} = {1}" -f $n, [Environment]::GetEnvironmentVariable($n,'User') }
"npm cache: $(npm config get cache 2>$null)"
H 'Git global (non-secret)'
git config --global --list 2>$null | Where-Object { $_ -notmatch 'token|password|credential' }
H 'Winget list (first 120 lines)'
winget list --accept-source-agreements 2>$null | Select-Object -First 120
H 'Drives'
Get-Volume | Where-Object DriveLetter | Select-Object DriveLetter,FileSystemLabel,@{n='SizeGB';e={[math]::Round($_.Size/1GB,1)}},@{n='FreeGB';e={[math]::Round($_.SizeRemaining/1GB,1)}} | Format-Table -AutoSize | Out-String
H 'Pagefile / hibernate / power'
Get-CimInstance Win32_PageFileSetting | Select-Object Name,InitialSize,MaximumSize | Format-Table -AutoSize | Out-String
Get-CimInstance Win32_PageFileUsage | Select-Object Name,AllocatedBaseSize,PeakUsage | Format-Table -AutoSize | Out-String
(Get-CimInstance Win32_ComputerSystem).AutomaticManagedPagefile
powercfg /a 2>&1 | Select-Object -First 6
H 'Google Drive / OneDrive'
Get-Process GoogleDriveFS,OneDrive -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue | Select-Object ProcessName,Id | Format-Table -AutoSize | Out-String
Get-PSDrive -PSProvider FileSystem | Select-Object Name,Root | Format-Table -AutoSize | Out-String
Get-ChildItem "$env:LOCALAPPDATA\Google\DriveFS" -Force -Directory | ForEach-Object { $_.Name }
Get-ItemProperty 'HKCU:\Software\Google\DriveFS\Share' -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue | Select-Object -Property * -ExcludeProperty PS* | Format-List | Out-String
Get-ItemProperty 'HKCU:\Software\Microsoft\OneDrive\Accounts\*' -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue | Select-Object UserFolder | Format-List | Out-String
H 'Startup entries'
Get-CimInstance Win32_StartupCommand | Select-Object Name,Location | Format-Table -AutoSize | Out-String
H 'Non-Microsoft scheduled tasks'
Get-ScheduledTask | Where-Object { $_.TaskPath -notmatch '^\\Microsoft' } | Select-Object TaskName,State,TaskPath | Format-Table -AutoSize | Out-String
H 'Defender exclusions (paths only)'
(Get-MpPreference).ExclusionPath
H 'Top C: users of space (profile level, shallow)'
Get-ChildItem C:\ -Force -Directory | Select-Object Name | ForEach-Object { $_.Name }
  ```

  ```
# Old Laptop Rebuild - Windows Prompts (Build 3 C Drive Cleanup and Build 4 Terminal Parity)

Two prompts for Claude Code sessions on the Dell's Windows side, written against the Sonnet 5.5 prompting guide (task, intent and constraints in the first turn; literal scoping; reasons next to rules; the "keep working" and "stop and report" paragraphs; a real-check verification rule; explicit approval rules for risky actions). A third, small prompt for the Acer is in the appendix.

## How to run
- **Where:** Claude Code in PowerShell on the Dell, started from `C:\Users\Anant Gupta`. Pick Sonnet 5.5 with `/model`.
- **Effort:** Build 3 at `high` (judgment, images, deletions). Build 4 at `medium` (well-specified installs and configs).
- **Order:** Build 3, then the Acer snapshot prompt (appendix), then Build 4. Build 4 can start without the snapshot, but its configs would then be rebuilt from notes and not byte-identical to the Acer's.
- **Images:** paste the seven WizTree screenshots (C: three, D: four) in the same message as the Build 3 prompt. Build 4 gets none.
- **Admin steps:** the sessions run without elevation and write every admin step to a script under `D:\WSL\ops\windows\` for you to run in an elevated PowerShell.
- **Numbering:** Build 1 was WSL, Build 2 was WSL VS Code plus the SSH workflow. These are Build 3 and Build 4. Windows VS Code and MCP wiring become Build 5, and the dev-only profile with the final check against the Acer becomes Build 6.

---

## Build 3 prompt: C: cleanup, Google Drive mirror, D: baseline, WSL host settings

~~~text
<situation>
You are Claude Code running in Windows PowerShell, started in C:\Users\Anant Gupta, on my old laptop: Dell Latitude 5530, Windows 11 Pro (build 26300), i7-1255U, 32 GB RAM, Intel Iris Xe graphics only, one 1 TB NVMe split into C: (251.9 GB) and D: (700 GB). This is Build 3 of a rebuild that makes this Dell match my newer Acer laptop for development work. Builds 1 and 2 finished the WSL side in separate sessions. This session covers the Windows side of storage: free up C:, fix what keeps refilling it, set up a clean D:, set up Google Drive mirroring for the files this laptop needs, and apply the Windows-only WSL host settings.
Why: C: is about 89% to 93% used and this laptop has had repeated crashes and failures that trace back to a full C:. A past cleanup made it worse (a "quarantine" folder moved files onto the same drive). Every step you take should say which drive it frees. Terminal installs and configs are the next build, and Windows VS Code and MCP wiring come after that, so leave both alone.
I attached seven WizTree screenshots from 2026-10-04: three of C: (tree view, expanded Users and Anant Gupta, and the pagefile selection) and four of D:. If they are missing from this message, say so in your first line and continue with live scans while I attach them.
</situation>

<facts>
Verified on 2026-10-11 from live queries, my notes and the screenshots. Treat them as a starting map and re-measure anything that drives a decision.
- Disks: C: 251.9 GB with 28.2 GB free; D: 700 GB with about 364 GB free. Known folders are redirected to D:\Users\_Anant (Documents is D:\Users\_Anant\10_Areas\Documents, Desktop is D:\Users\_Anant\Inbox, Downloads is D:\Users\_Anant\Downloads). My Jarvis and The Plan Obsidian vaults live under D:\Users\_Anant and are synced by Syncthing. Google Drive must never cover them.
- Pagefile: Windows is configured for d:\pagefile.sys (size 0/0, automatic management off), but that file does not exist. The live one is C:\pagefile.sys at about 26 to 31 GB with a peak use of 1 to 6 GB. My hypothesis is that Windows cannot create the D: file at boot, falls back to a RAM-sized file on C:, and shows the pagefile warning at startup. Confirm in the event log before changing anything.
- WizTree on C: (2026-10-04): Users 128 GB (AppData 100 GB: Local 67.8 and Roaming 32.2), Windows 39.4, pagefile.sys 25.6, Program Files 26.2, ProgramData 5.8, Program Files (x86) 8.1, System Volume Information 4.2, opamroot 2.4, Python313 0.3, srverify-20260502173203 0.24. By type: .dll 47.8 GB, .sys 26.8, .exe 25.8, .vhdx 11.7 (15 files), .gz 6.3, .gguf 4.7 (3 files), .dmp 4.3 (57 files), .jar 3.2, .msi 3.1 (mostly C:\Windows\Installer, which I do not touch), .onnx 1.6.
- Measured AppData\Local (GiB, 2026-10-07): Programs 14.1, Spotify 6.4, Vivaldi 5.6, Microsoft 4.9, superwhisper 4.8, npm-cache 4.3, Packages 3.7, Temp 3.4, uv 2.5, hermes 2.1, WisprFlow 1.9, ms-playwright 1.3. Roaming (WizTree 10-04): Claude 10.7 (likely Claude Desktop's VM bundle, which explains the .vhdx files), Code 3.4, Jan 3.2 (the .gguf models), Cursor 2.8, Kiro 2.6, npm 1.7. Profile folders: miniconda3 7.1, vscode-remote-wsl 6.3, .vscode 4.1, .codex 3.4, [REDACTED] 2.3, .cache 1.4. Local\Google reports a bogus size because of Drive virtual files, so skip it when measuring.
- Profile root also holds stray items to classify: 0K7YY.exe, nc6a9.exe, a file named NUL, a file named echo, global.yml, settings.json, battery-report.html, mongosh files, scikit_learn_data, and C:\Users\Anant Gupta\bin.
- WizTree on D: (2026-10-04): WSL 176 GB (Ubuntu ext4.vhdx 105, Installers\wsl-ubuntu.tar 36.7, Docker 33.9), Users 111.5 (Games 86.0 including a 67 GB game archive, _Anant 25.4), $RECYCLE.BIN 32.5 (about 21 GB of Rust target directories), AI\ollama-models 9.2, WindowsApps 3.6, npm-cache 1.6, WpSystem 0.8.
- C: leftovers from WSL work: vscode-remote-wsl 6.3 GiB, the 2026-09-20 quarantine 2.3 GiB, %TEMP%\wsl-crashes 0.5 GiB (three process-level core dumps, no VM corruption).
- Tool caches still defaulting to C: because the env vars are unset: UV_CACHE_DIR, PIP_CACHE_DIR, PLAYWRIGHT_BROWSERS_PATH. The npm cache is already D:\npm-cache, but a stale 4.3 GiB copy sits in AppData\Local\npm-cache.
- WSL (finished by Build 1): version 3.0.1.0, .wslconfig on C: has networkingMode=mirrored, firewall=true, memory=20GB, processors=10, swap=8GB, swapfile=D:\\WSL\\swap.vhdx, vmIdleTimeout=-1, instanceIdleTimeout=-1, autoMemoryReclaim=gradual, sparseVhd=true. Ubuntu is at D:\WSL\Ubuntu, Docker Desktop's disk is under D:\WSL\Docker, and Docker Desktop is not running. This laptop is the always-on SSH host for the Acer, so WSL must stay up and reachable. Tailscale lives only inside this laptop's WSL and is never installed on its Windows side.
- A hidden scheduled task ConversationCapture-Backfill-WSL runs wsl.exe every 30 minutes. Other non-Microsoft tasks: ConversationCapture-Backfill-Windows, Jarvis-GitAutoSync, Jarvis-Syncthing-Health, Jarvis-WeeklyReview, Jarvis-Cursor-Session-Export, Syncthing, two disabled sync tasks, a dubbing-app login task. Startup entries include Ollama, OpenClaw Gateway, Wispr Flow, Discord, RiotClient, Medal, Steam, Rize, Spotify, Docker Desktop, Voquill, Slack, DubbingAI, E START App and GoogleDriveFS.
- Google Drive for desktop 132 is installed. The DriveFS folder holds three account folders, no G: drive is mounted, and no GoogleDriveFS process was running at the time of the check. OneDrive has no folder configured.
- Installed apps worth reviewing (partial winget list): IntelliJ IDEA Ultimate 2024.3, MongoDB Shell, two Malwarebytes entries, Respondus LockDown Browser, Dell Optimizer, SupportAssist plugin, Microsoft 365 Apps, Docker Desktop 4.56, Miniconda, Python 3.13 in C:\Python313, Node 22, Jan, Ollama, Postman, Cluely, Loom, Netflix, FFmpeg, opam, Steam, Riot, Medal, Bitwarden. Chocolatey is also installed.
- Settings Sync is live between this laptop and the Acer. Anything written to a Windows user-level VS Code file here reaches the Acer within minutes.
</facts>

<reading_list>
Read these in full, in this order, before acting. They are under D:\Users\_Anant\10_Areas\Documents\Jarvis\40_Resources\CS\Concepts\New Laptop\ (also reachable through the jarvis MCP if it is connected). Read the vault's AGENTS.md before writing there.
1. Old Laptop Rebuild\C Drive Bloat - Failure Log and Prevention Rules (the root causes and growth rules for this exact problem).
2. Old Laptop Rebuild\Old Laptop Rebuild - Index, Build 1 WSL Findings (its handoffs), and Build 2 VS Code and Two-Laptop Findings (its Windows-side findings).
3. Installations (the install-placement buckets A, B and C, the "not carrying forward" list, the drive placement table), New Laptop Setup, Old Laptop Decommission Checklist.
4. Sync\Google Drive Sync Policy, then the Sync folder's Operations Reference, Known Failure Modes and Prevention, and Build Roadmap, to learn exactly where Google Drive stops and Syncthing starts. Also Acer Live State (the G: drive, the C:\Data mount point, OneDrive Known Folder Move).
5. Ubuntu - WSL (Mistakes already made once, Maintenance Cadence).
If I paste older incident notes into this session, read them before the notes above. The vault can be stale, and the live machine wins; log each contradiction.
</reading_list>

<decisions_already_made>
Carry these without reopening them.
- WSL, Docker's WSL disk and the WSL swap file live on D:. Tailscale is never installed on this laptop's Windows side. Mirrored networking stays.
- Electron and Microsoft Store apps cannot choose an install location. They stay on C: and get their caches cleaned. Apps whose installers offer a real location choice go to D:\Apps\<Name> through winget --location or a manual installer.
- Google Drive on this Dell uses Mirror mode and on the Acer uses Stream mode. Drive carries documents and other non-regenerable files only: no code, no .git, no node_modules or build output, no AI-tool config directories, no secrets, and no Obsidian vault. Drive cannot exclude subfolders inside a folder that is already selected, so separate folders at the top level instead.
- A quarantine folder lives on D: and has a review date in its name.
- Windows VS Code user files and the Settings Sync state are out of bounds for this session.
</decisions_already_made>

<task>
Work in these phases, in order. Finish and check each phase before starting the next. Keep working until everything I asked for is done, and stop to ask only when you cannot go on without me or before a risky step. When the work is done and checked, stop and report. Do not add features, files, docs or refactors that I did not ask for; mention a good idea at the end instead.

Phase 1, inventory (read-only).
1. Transcribe the visible numbers from the screenshots into a table (path, size, allocated, file count). Where a figure is hard to read, crop and zoom the image with a small Python or PowerShell script before you trust it. Run any Python that reads files I give you with `python -I`.
2. Re-measure every figure that will drive a decision with live scans of C: and D: (use per-folder scans with Get-ChildItem or du-style sums, skipping reparse points; WizTree is the map and the live scan is the truth). Report drift against the screenshots.
3. Find the real causes of the crashes: Application and System event log errors since the last boot and the past 30 days, WER reports, and the 57 .dmp files (locate them, group by program, keep the newest one). Name the three most frequent crashing programs.
4. Find why the D: pagefile does not get created. Test these in order: the System log around boot, BitLocker state of D: (`manage-bde -status D:`), whether D: is NTFS and healthy, whether D: mounts after the pagefile is initialized, and any storage-redirect setting ("where new content is saved"). Search current Microsoft documentation for the sizing and crash-dump rules before proposing numbers.
5. List every installed program with its install location and size, and classify each as keep, remove, reinstall on D:, or forced to C:, using the Installations buckets. Flag programs on the "not carrying forward" list.
6. Read the Google Drive state: which of the three accounts is which (by folder metadata and sign-in state, never by reading file contents), and what mode Drive is in.

Phase 2, plan and checkpoint. Write the findings to the vault (see logging), then stop and show me:
- a table of what is on C: and D:, by size, with a verdict per item;
- Tier A, Tier B and Tier C lists (see authorization) with sizes and the drive each frees;
- the pagefile root cause and the exact configuration you recommend, with the numbers justified;
- the crash causes and the program fixes;
- the Google Drive design: which top-level folders mirror, where the mirror folder lives, and why the Jarvis and The Plan vaults stay out;
- the startup and scheduled-task review, with a recommendation per item.
Wait for my reply.

Phase 3, execute Tier A after I reply "go A" (or edit the list). Delete the regenerable items with each tool's own method where one exists (npm, uv, pip and Playwright cache commands, Storage Sense or cleanmgr-equivalent, DISM StartComponentCleanup in an admin script). Measure C: free space after every group.

Phase 4, fix the causes.
1. Set the user env vars UV_CACHE_DIR, PIP_CACHE_DIR and PLAYWRIGHT_BROWSERS_PATH to folders on D:, confirm each tool uses them, and delete the old C: copies in the same step.
2. Write the elevated script for the pagefile change and for anything else that needs admin, under D:\WSL\ops\windows\, with a backup and rollback printed first. Give me the single command to run it.
3. Decide with me what happens to ConversationCapture-Backfill-WSL now that WSL stays up all the time, and write the logon task that starts WSL if the host needs one.

Phase 5, apps and profile clutter. Work through the Tier B list one item at a time, after my approval of each item or group.

Phase 6, Google Drive mirror. Check the official Drive for desktop documentation for Mirror mode and the local folder location, then set it up as designed in Phase 2, with the mirror folder on D:. Sign in or out of an account only after I say so. Check afterwards that the vaults, .git folders, node_modules and secret files are not inside the mirrored set.

Phase 7, D: baseline and Windows host settings.
1. Review D: with me (the 36.7 GB installer tarball, the game archive, the recycle-bin contents, Docker data, model files), then create the standard folders the notes call for and nothing more.
2. Verify the WSL host state from Windows: `wsl --version`, `wsl -l -v`, .wslconfig contents, the swap file on D:, the Ubuntu and Docker disks, and the sparse-disk status.
3. Write a Defender exclusion script for the WSL and Docker disks and the D: dev folders as an option, and explain the security tradeoff before I run it.
4. Review startup entries and scheduled tasks one by one and apply only the ones I approve.

Phase 8, final check and logging. Re-measure C: and D:, run the checks from the Growth rules table in the failure-log note, and write the results.
</task>

<authorization>

</sensitive_data>
  ```

  ```
# Old Laptop Rebuild - Windows Prompts (Build 3 C Drive Cleanup and Build 4 Terminal Parity)

Two prompts for Claude Code sessions on the Dell's Windows side, written against the Sonnet 5.5 prompting guide (task, intent and constraints in the first turn; literal scoping; reasons next to rules; the "keep working" and "stop and report" paragraphs; a real-check verification rule; explicit approval rules for risky actions). A third, small prompt for the Acer is in the appendix.

## How to run
- **Where:** Claude Code in PowerShell on the Dell, started from `C:\Users\Anant Gupta`. Pick Sonnet 5.5 with `/model`.
- **Effort:** Build 3 at `high` (judgment, images, deletions). Build 4 at `medium` (well-specified installs and configs).
- **Order:** Build 3, then the Acer snapshot prompt (appendix), then Build 4. Build 4 can start without the snapshot, but its configs would then be rebuilt from notes and not byte-identical to the Acer's.
- **Images:** paste the seven WizTree screenshots (C: three, D: four) in the same message as the Build 3 prompt. Build 4 gets none.
- **Admin steps:** the sessions run without elevation and write every admin step to a script under `D:\WSL\ops\windows\` for you to run in an elevated PowerShell.
- **Numbering:** Build 1 was WSL, Build 2 was WSL VS Code plus the SSH workflow. These are Build 3 and Build 4. Windows VS Code and MCP wiring become Build 5, and the dev-only profile with the final check against the Acer becomes Build 6.

---

## Build 3 prompt: C: cleanup, Google Drive mirror, D: baseline, WSL host settings

~~~text
<situation>
You are Claude Code running in Windows PowerShell, started in C:\Users\Anant Gupta, on my old laptop: Dell Latitude 5530, Windows 11 Pro (build 26300), i7-1255U, 32 GB RAM, Intel Iris Xe graphics only, one 1 TB NVMe split into C: (251.9 GB) and D: (700 GB). This is Build 3 of a rebuild that makes this Dell match my newer Acer laptop for development work. Builds 1 and 2 finished the WSL side in separate sessions. This session covers the Windows side of storage: free up C:, fix what keeps refilling it, set up a clean D:, set up Google Drive mirroring for the files this laptop needs, and apply the Windows-only WSL host settings.
Why: C: is about 89% to 93% used and this laptop has had repeated crashes and failures that trace back to a full C:. A past cleanup made it worse (a "quarantine" folder moved files onto the same drive). Every step you take should say which drive it frees. Terminal installs and configs are the next build, and Windows VS Code and MCP wiring come after that, so leave both alone.
I attached seven WizTree screenshots from 2026-10-04: three of C: (tree view, expanded Users and Anant Gupta, and the pagefile selection) and four of D:. If they are missing from this message, say so in your first line and continue with live scans while I attach them.
  ```

## Old Laptop section
I added a `## Old Laptop` section to `Installations.md`, labelled `Old Laptop: 40_Resources/CS/Concepts/New Laptop/Installations`. It comes from live queries on the Dell on 2026-10-11, reconciled with the Build 1, 2 and 3 findings:
- **Windows side:** shell and terminal, CLI tools and runtimes, AI platforms and models, apps that can't leave C:, and Dell and Microsoft packages. A pending-removal marker tracks what Build 3 plans to remove.
- **WSL side:** apt packages, the user-space binaries in `~/.local/bin`, runtimes, global packages, AI CLIs, tmux plugins and the 34 editor extensions.
- **Config parity:** the Dell's WSL configs are mostly reconstructions. 13 of 17 comparable files differ from the Acer's real ones, 4 Yazi plugin files match, and 3 Yazi files are missing.
- **Surprises it records:**
  - Ollama is installed inside the Dell's WSL, against the "Windows-side only" decision.
  - PowerShell 7.6 is installed in the Dell's WSL, and no build note records who installed it.
  - The Acer's `starship.toml` is identical on Windows and WSL apart from CRLF line endings in the WSL copy.

## Build 4, rewritten (run at high effort, in the Windows home directory)
- **Both laptops are in scope.** The Acer snapshot confirms its Windows side is incomplete too: Windows Terminal is mostly on defaults, there is no PowerShell profile, and its git config is minimal.
- **Script-first.** The session writes the scripts and you run them: Dell first, then the Acer from the same vault folder, which Syncthing carries. The session itself only does read-only checks and dry runs. For every script, and for each command that needs a UAC prompt or a login, it gives you the exact command to paste.
- **One manifest drives everything.** `toolkit.json` feeds the install script, the verify script and the catalog generator. The scripts detect the vault path themselves, because the Dell's and the Acer's differ. A version lock file from the Dell run keeps the Acer on the same versions.
- **Parity.**
  - The Acer's real config files are the source.
  - Config files are written as UTF-8 with LF endings and hash-checked.
  - The Dell's reconstructed WSL configs get replaced by the snapshot's files, with backups. Machine-specific files such as `.wslconfig`, the `gh-dash` repo paths and the git identity are left alone.
- **Catalog for AI agents.** It produces a per-machine tool catalog covering what's installed, its version, path and drive, plus an authored guide that answers your "tell me exactly how to use it and where". The guide has an agent front page (which shell for what, where projects live, where the vault is, the sync boundaries, secrets rules) and a card per tool grouped by task. A pointer for the Windows home `AGENTS.md` is proposed but not written.
- **MCP prerequisites** are checked by name only: npx, uvx, `mcp-remote`, Docker, and whether each configured server's launch command resolves on the machine.
- **Guardrails stay:** no Tailscale on the Dell's Windows side, no VS Code or MCP registry files, and no `wsl --shutdown`.
- **Checkpoints.** It stops after the audit and design for decisions: Node 22 or LTS, the pip-installed uv versus the standalone one, where Miniconda goes, `core.autocrlf`, the Windows Terminal scheme and font, and whether to keep Ollama and PowerShell 7 inside the Dell's WSL.

I also updated the Index and the intro and "How to run" lines of the Windows prompts note, so they no longer point at the old appendix.

## Things to check
- **The earlier Windows Terminal note was wrong.** The Acer's real terminal is on defaults, not the Tokyo Night setup my earlier prompt assumed. Build 4 proposes Tokyo Night Storm to match Starship, Yazi and tmux, but you decide.
- **The Acer snapshot isn't fully finished.** It has no `MANIFEST.md` or `VERSIONS.md` yet, so the Acer session may still be working. Build 4 reads whatever exists.
- **Build 3 is mid-way.** Its audit found two byte-identical executables in your profile root that look like malware droppers, and it's waiting on your "go A". Run its install-heavy steps before Build 4's installs so C: has room.

Send me each session's output when it finishes, and I'll write the follow-ups into this note.

  ```
# Old Laptop Rebuild - Build 3 Windows Cleanup Findings

## One-Line Answer
==Phase 1 (read-only) is done. C: already sits at 39.6 GiB free because the Revo cleanup ran before this session; the pagefile is the biggest single item left (25.4 GiB on C:), and its D: replacement is blocked for a reason Windows does not log, so the fix is an explicit sized pair of pagefiles proven by a reboot test, not another guess.==

### Suspicious files in the profile root
`0K7YY.exe` and `nc6a9.exe` are byte-identical (14,115,592 bytes, SHA-256 `[REDACTED]`), dated 2025-01-20. Both report ProductName "MoUsoCoreWorker" (a Windows Update process name), have a Chinese company certificate subject (Zhejiang, Hangzhou), and the Authenticode status is **HashMismatch**, which means the file was altered after signing. Random 5-character names plus a spoofed Windows product name is a malware-dropper pattern. They were not run. Recommendation: do not execute, run a Defender full scan, then delete with approval. Hash lookup on VirusTotal sends only the hash; I will do it only if you say so.
`NUL` (903 bytes) is listed by `Get-ChildItem` but `Test-Path` on the `\\?\` form said False, so removal needs the exact name; Tier B. `echo` (0 bytes) exists. `global.yml` is 0 bytes, `settings.json` 124 bytes, `battery-report.html` 0.2 MB, `mongosh-2.5.9-x64.msi` 0.05 GiB.

## Pagefile root cause and recommended configuration
Evidence:
- D: is NTFS, Healthy, Basic partition 5 on the same disk, and Ntfs event 98 reports it healthy at 13:04:56, one second after boot. A failed mount is not the cause.
- BitLocker: the shell property `System.Volume.BitLockerProtection` is 1 (on) for both C: and D:. Boot logs show BitLocker event 24641 ("unexpected error ... retrieve the volume master key during restart"), 24 times in 30 days, and event 24711 (V2 TPM protector used). `manage-bde` needs admin, so D:'s protector type and auto-unlock state are not read yet. That is the first admin step.
- Registry `PagingFiles` is `d:\pagefile.sys 0 0` (system managed on D: only) and `ExistingPageFiles` lists only C:.
- Crash dump type is 3 (small dump). Microsoft states dump features need a pagefile on the boot volume, so a configuration with nothing on C: makes Windows create its own fallback file on C:.
- No System-log event names a failure to create the D: file.
Verdict: **probable, not proven**: two causes stack, (1) the boot-volume rule forces a C: file when none is configured, and (2) D: is BitLocker-protected and may not be unlocked when the pagefile is initialized. Only the elevated diagnostic can separate them.
Sizing: Microsoft sizes by peak commit charge and the dump type, not by RAM. Live commit is **43.2 GiB of a 57.1 GiB limit** (RAM 31.7 GiB plus 25.4 GiB pagefile). Peak pagefile use (1.2 GiB now, 6.5 GiB on 10-07) is low, but the commit limit matters: Windows logged 38 low-virtual-memory events (ID 2004) in 30 days, 27 of them on 10-10, and all 38 named **vmmemWSL** as the top consumer (about 16 GiB). Shrinking the pagefile to match "peak use" would cut commit headroom and cause crashes.
Recommendation (after the diagnostic confirms D: can host it):
- `C:\pagefile.sys` fixed 4,096 MB initial and maximum. Satisfies the boot-volume rule for a small dump and gives a safe fallback.
- `D:\pagefile.sys` fixed 32,768 MB initial and maximum.
- Commit limit becomes about 68 GiB (31.7 + 36), above today's 57.1, and C: gains about 21 GiB. Keep crash dump type at small.
- Verify after one reboot: `Win32_PageFileUsage` must list both files and `D:\pagefile.sys` must exist. If D: is missing again, rollback is C: fixed 32,768 MB and remove the D: entry; C: then gains nothing but stops the RAM-sized fallback.
The elevated script and rollback are written in Phase 4. The read-only diagnostic exists now: `D:\WSL\ops\windows\diag-pagefile-bitlocker.ps1`.

## Crash causes
Window: last 30 days (Kernel-Power 41, BugCheck 1001 and 6008 events: none, so there were no blue screens or hard power loss).
| Rank | Program | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | VS Code | 15 Crashpad reports (Jul 3, Aug 4, Sep 5, Oct 3; newest 10-03), 0.45 GiB |
| 2 | Realtek audio service `RtkAudUService64.exe` | 4 Application Error events in 30 d plus 1 dump |
| 3 | Loom | 3 dumps in `CrashDumps` (WizTree64 has 2 dumps and 2 error events, so it ties) |
Other Application errors: CodexSandboxService.OpenAI.Codex 16 (service errors, 2 since boot), VSS 12, Universal Print 10, `wslservice.exe` 1, `explorer.exe` 1, `svchost.exe` 1. System errors: DistributedCOM 10001 x82, HAL 20/21 x31 each (RTC query fails, firmware), BitLocker 24641 x24, Hyper-V VmSwitch 15 x21, Windows Update 20 x17.
The pattern that matches "crashes tied to a full C:" is the commit exhaustion above (vmmemWSL), not bugchecks.
Program fixes: update or reinstall the Realtek audio driver from Dell; Loom and WizTree are low-value (remove Loom if unused, update WizTree); keep one VS Code Crashpad report and rely on Settings Sync-safe checks later; investigate the Codex sandbox service error text before touching it.
dump inventory: 37 .dmp on C: (1.22 GiB): wsl-crashes 3 (0.54), Code 15 (0.45), Kiro 3 (0.10), CrashDumps 10 (0.09), Todoist 1, WebView 1, Vivaldi 4. None found outside the profile (no `C:\Windows\Minidump` or `MEMORY.DMP`).

## Wipe list handling (Copilot, Office, OneDrive, Edge, Bing, tracking)
Plan only, nothing run:
- Copilot: package already absent; still add the policy value that turns the Copilot entry points off (admin script).
- Microsoft 365: Office uninstall through Revo or the Office installer, then its leftover Appx entries.
- OneDrive: no process is running and no folder is configured; confirm `OneDrive.exe` under `C:\Windows\SysWOW64` and remove it with the documented `OneDriveSetup.exe /uninstall`; known folders stay redirected to D:.
- Default browser and Edge: Windows 11 in this region does not allow uninstalling Edge, and WebView2 is a dependency of Obsidian, Teams, Claude and Cursor tooling. Set Vivaldi as default for http, https, .html and PDF (Windows requires you to do this in Settings, one click per type), and add policy keys that stop Edge from starting at logon and prompting.
- Bing and web search: remove BingSearch, BingNews, BingWeather and set the policy `DisableSearchBoxSuggestions` plus `BingSearchEnabled=0` for the user.
- Tracking: Windows 11 Pro allows telemetry level "Required" (1), not 0. Set `AllowTelemetry=1`, turn off advertising ID, tailored experiences, activity history upload, and disable DiagTrack only if you accept losing Windows Update diagnostics. Needs your approval per group.

Design (applies once you approve):
- Mode: Mirror, with the mirror root on D: (path decided in Phase 6 from the official docs).
- Mirror these top-level folders only: `D:\_Anant\00_Inbox`, `20_Progress`, `30_Resources`, plus the document-only `UMN-Documents`, `Finance`, `Job & Apartment` folders as the Policy note lists. No code, no `.git`, no `node_modules`, no AI config, no secrets.
- Out: Jarvis and The Plan (Syncthing owns them, and Drive plus Syncthing on the same files would produce conflict copies), `D:\Users\_Anant\10_Areas\Documents` (it contains the Jarvis vault), `D:\WSL`, `D:\AI`, `C:\Users\Anant Gupta`.

- DriveFS cache stays on C: (no supported redirect per the Acer notes).
  ```

# Old Laptop Rebuild - Build 2 VS Code and Two-Laptop Findings

## One-Line Answer
==WSL-side VS Code and user-level developer safety parity are applied and verified; Tailscale is now live on Dell WSL and Acer Windows, with direct ping evidence in both directions. The Dell host remains degraded on systemd-binfmt, and Acer SSH host-key verification and Remote-SSH checks are incomplete.==

## Scope and status
Phase 2 items 1 through 6 are applied. The user has since installed/authenticated Tailscale on Dell Ubuntu WSL and Acer Windows and enabled Dell Tailscale SSH. No repo was modified. No Windows user-level VS Code file, Settings Sync control, WSL configuration, SSH server, editor-server data, or Miniconda installation was changed. Tailscale is the sole network component enabled so far. Phase 4 is in progress but paused at the systemd decision gate; admin-console policy/key-expiry and Acer SSH/Remote-SSH verification remain incomplete.

## Before and after
| Area | Before | After / evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Remote WSL settings | Machine settings file absent | `~/.vscode-server/data/Machine/settings.json` created; JSON read-back valid; bash profile, Python activation, Linux interpreter, Jupyter exclusions, watcher exclusions, sandbox off |
| WSL extensions | 25 | 34 target extensions; stale entries removed, full C/C++ pack removed intentionally; final CLI count 34 |
| Shell environment | No vscode-env files/block | init, template, guarded Bash block, home workspace env/settings created; `bash -n` passed |
| Warm shell startup, 5 runs | Disabled: 0.44, 0.34, 0.34, 0.35, 0.36 sec | Enabled: 0.34, 0.38, 0.37, 0.35, 0.34 sec; median 0.35 sec both |
| Global Git config | Six requested keys absent | pull.rebase true; push.autoSetupRemote true; fetch.prune true; rerere.enabled true; core.autocrlf input; init.defaultBranch main |
| Global Git ignore | Absent | Eight named secret patterns installed and read back |
| Claude read safety | Two existing deny rules | Existing rules preserved; Acer-named file/path Read denials added; JSON valid |
| Claude user MCPs | jarvis, the-plan, jarvis-fs were project scope only | Three user-scope entries; `cd /tmp && claude mcp list` reports all connected |
| System-level packages | bubblewrap/socat absent | Not installed; `/mnt/d/WSL/ops/build2-sudo-step.sh` prepared for user, not run |
| Miniconda | No `~/miniconda3` directory found | Not installed; root has 902 GiB free and D: 364 GiB free. Rebuild cost is not estimated because the package-list dry-run from the referenced WSL note has not been performed; retain approval gate. |
| Tailscale | Not installed at Build 2 checkpoint | User installed/authenticated on Dell WSL and Acer Windows. Dell `tailscaled` active; `dell-wsl.tail0c1d06.ts.net`, `RunSSH=true`, Tailscale 1.104.1 stable; admin console shows key expiry disabled; peer ping to Acer direct. |

Backups made before edits: `~/.bashrc.build2-20261007.bak`, `~/.gitconfig.build2-20261007.bak`, `~/.claude/settings.json.build2-20261007.bak`, `~/.claude.json.build2-20261007.bak`, and `~/.mcp.json.build2-20261007.bak`. New target files had no previous version. JSON validation (`jq empty`) and shell syntax validation (`bash -n`) passed. Existing `gh auth git-credential` remains; no plaintext credential helper was added. Secrets were not printed.

## State-changing commands and errors
Commands applied in WSL after dated backups:

## Build 1 gate and host status
The user's corrected idle-test output is PASS: after Ubuntu was started, the test made no WSL call for 75 seconds, then `wsl -l --running --quiet` listed Ubuntu. This exceeds the default 60-second VM idle timer. The prior FAIL was the Windows PowerShell 5.1 output decoding bug documented in Build 1 findings, not a stopped distro. The sparse conversion refusal was Microsoft’s potential-corruption safeguard. The host script design incorrectly made an optional sparse conversion fatal; the safe refusal is now recorded as SKIPPED and independent checks continue.

The post-update host report checked config/start, memory, processors, swap, and `wslview`; `wslview --version` works. It counted zero `CheckConnection` journal events over 120 seconds. On 2026-10-11, Phase 4.1 read-only recheck returned `systemctl is-system-running=degraded`; the sole failed unit is `systemd-binfmt.service`, `Result=exit-code`, `ExecMainStatus=1`. The live journal gives the exact line `Failed to flush binfmt_misc rules, ignoring: Read-only file system`. The unit then exits nonzero. Its current one-shot operation is to reset and register Linux `binfmt_misc` handlers. Microsoft issue [WSL #41783](https://github.com/microsoft/WSL/issues/41783) documents this exact WSL 3.0.1 behavior: systemd tries to clear handlers by writing `-1` to `/proc/sys/fs/binfmt_misc/status`; WSL rejects that flush because the registry is kernel-global/read-only in this context, then the unit exits with an error. A Microsoft WSL maintainer closed the issue as fixed in WSL 3.0.2. The [3.0.2 release listing](https://github.com/microsoft/WSL/releases/tag/3.0.2) currently labels it pre-release. No workaround was attempted; the user chose to wait for a stable release.

The practical current impact is bounded and measured: systemd PID 1 is still managing the distro, but reports `degraded`; `systemd-binfmt.service` is a one-shot boot setup unit, not a long-running daemon. The failed operation is its attempt to flush all binfmt rules. Microsoft's reproducer says configured rules are then registered but the unit still exits nonzero. This Dell's kernel currently shows enabled `WSLInterop` (`/init`, `MZ`) and `python3.12` (`/usr/bin/python3.12`) handlers, and the only binfmt config found was the Python 3.12 registration. `cmd.exe /c ver` succeeded, native Python launched successfully, and earlier `wslview --version` passed. No current loss of Windows interop, native Linux executable launch, or configured Python handler was observed. A future workload depending on some other custom format handler could be affected if its registration is absent, but none is configured here. This failure does not mean the WSL distro, SSH/Tailscale daemon, or ordinary Linux processes have stopped. The `cmd.exe` smoke test printed the normal UNC-current-directory warning and then defaulted to the Windows directory; command exit was 0, unrelated to binfmt.

Build 1's corrected 75-second idle test passed, but Phase 4.5 must repeat after service configuration and every WSL/editor/Docker/Codex session is closed. Phase 4 is paused until stable WSL contains the fix; do not clear the failure, make a service override, or install a prerelease workaround.

User-provided Acer evidence: `tailscale ping dell-wsl` returned `pong ... via 10.5.8.253:41641 in 54ms`, which is the direct endpoint form, not `via DERP(...)`. Acer Windows OpenSSH then displayed a first-connect prompt for `dell-wsl (100.94.129.113)` with ED25519 fingerprint `SHA256:[REDACTED]`. The user has not reported accepting it or completing authentication. The host-key fingerprint is unverified: do not accept until compared against the Dell host key distributed through Tailscale's control plane. This observed prompt resolves part of the earlier uncertainty: plain Windows `ssh.exe` did prompt on this first connection despite the docs' general managed-known-hosts description. It does not prove an attack; exact cause is unknown. No `hostname`, `pwd`, Git status, or Remote-SSH test has completed.

Exact Acer terminal output provided by the user:
```text
tailscale ping dell-wsl
```
