The measured Dell interface MTUs are `tailscale0=1280`, `eth0=1500`, `eth1=1500`, `loopback0=1500`. Default route is via `eth1`. No MTU was changed. Tailscale documents a WSL adjustment from 1280 to 1340 in a specific default-interface condition; the observed `tailscale0` value alone is not proof that the documented condition applies here. Follow the documented diagnostic on the relevant interface and test data-path sizes; do not guess a manual MTU.

## Questions Q1 to Q6

### Q1. Acer Windows Tailscale to Dell WSL Tailscale SSH
Tailscale SSH documentation says a tailnet device can connect to a Linux SSH server using the node's MagicDNS name or Tailscale IP. It uses tailnet node identity and control-plane-distributed host keys, with Tailscale client integration arranging known-host handling. Windows is a supported Tailscale client platform. Thus Windows-side Tailscale supplies the route to plain Windows `ssh.exe`; VS Code Remote-SSH can use that executable without a wrapper or ProxyCommand. Use `ssh anant_gupta@dell-wsl` after the node is named `dell-wsl`; if MagicDNS does not resolve, use the live `tailscale ip -4` address. The fully-qualified `.ts.net` name can be read from the admin device page if desired.

The Acer test now supplies the first-connect evidence: stock Windows `ssh.exe` displayed a normal unknown ED25519 host-key prompt for `dell-wsl (100.94.129.113)` with fingerprint `SHA256:[REDACTED]`. The user has not accepted it. This differs from the docs' general managed-known-host description; the cause is not established. Do not accept until matching the fingerprint against the host key advertised by Tailscale's control plane. Once that is done, re-run the plain `ssh` test. The optional Acer WSL terminal route remains untested. No wrapper, ProxyCommand, or Acer-WSL Tailscale is proposed. Sources: [Tailscale SSH](https://tailscale.com/docs/features/tailscale-ssh), [Tailscale WSL 2](https://tailscale.com/docs/install/windows/wsl2).

### Q2. Tailscale inside mirrored WSL
**PARTIAL.** Tailscale is active in Dell mirrored WSL and Acer Windows. User evidence shows direct Tailscale ping in both directions. Dell `tailscale0` is MTU 1280; other active interfaces are 1500. The end-to-end SSH and large transfer checks remain incomplete, so mirrored-mode Tailscale functionality is not fully verified. No manual MTU change was made. Follow Tailscale's documented check for the default interface and verify actual data transfer before deciding whether there is a defect.

### Q3. Personal tailnet SSH ACL
**PENDING USER CONSOLE CHECK.** Do not assume the personal tailnet's current policy either allows or blocks SSH. User must open Tailscale Admin Console → Access controls and inspect the existing policy's `ssh` rules. If needed, add only the smallest rule the user directs. Do not broaden or edit the policy without explicit direction. The Acer's SSH client currently shows an unverified host-key prompt, but no accepted-key/authentication result.

This is a correction to propose in the locked note before dependency configuration is approved. Source: [pnpm 10 settings](https://pnpm.io/10.x/settings), [pnpm node_modules settings](https://pnpm.io/settings/node-modules).

## WSL 3.0.1, mirrored network, sparse conversion
The live WSL is 3.0.1.0, kernel `6.18.40.1-1`, WSLg `1.0.79`. Current `.wslconfig` has `[wsl2] vmIdleTimeout=-1`, `[general] instanceIdleTimeout=-1`, `[experimental] autoMemoryReclaim=gradual`, `sparseVhd=true`, and `networkingMode=mirrored`. Microsoft's current [WSL configuration reference](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/wsl/wsl-config) documents those keys in those sections. The host script's post-update start/config check passed, and resource/swap checks matched configuration. This is evidence the live distro accepted the configuration sufficiently to start and operate, though the 3.0.1 release notes do not individually certify each key. See [WSL 3.0.1 release notes](https://github.com/microsoft/WSL/releases/tag/3.0.1).

`wsl.exe --manage --help` is invalid syntax. `wsl.exe --help` lists `--set-sparse` and `--compact`, but does not list `--allow-unsafe`. The safe conversion output explicitly says sparse VHD support is disabled due to potential corruption and describes `--allow-unsafe` as a force override. This proves the current request was guarded on this machine; it does not prove whether that guard is build-specific or a standing policy. Never recommend the override.

Mirrored-mode parity on WSL 3.0.1 is partially established: Dell-to-Acer and Acer-to-Dell Tailscale pings both returned direct endpoint paths (IP:port, not `DERP(...)`). SSH authentication, large transfer behavior, and a completed SSH remote session are still pending, so do not mark the workflow fully verified. The Dell `tailscale0` reports MTU 1280; `eth0` and `eth1` report 1500, and default route is via `eth1`. No guessed MTU change was made. Follow the [documented WSL check](https://tailscale.com/docs/install/windows/wsl2) on its stated interface and verify with an actual data transfer. `CheckConnection` was 0 over one 120-second sample, compared to Build 1's 5/hour old-build sample. This is not enough for a trend or causal conclusion. Treat a reduced rate as a hypothesis until a comparable longer sample is taken; do not change networking based on this single reading.

The pre-sync backup showed the old PowerShell default shell, Python activation false, PowerShell/cmd/Git Bash profiles, Linux remote SSH host, hybrid auto-forwarding, format-on-save, Biome default formatter, pnpm package manager, and trust setting. `settingsSync.ignoredSettings` is absent from the current settings file.

Actual WSLENV is not empty as the 2026-10-04 snapshot claimed: current decoded Windows value is `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE/w:`. This is a live contradiction to carry into Build 3. Microsoft documents machine-scoped `machine` and `machine-overridable` settings as excluded from sync and remote extensions as not synced ([VS Code Settings Sync](https://code.visualstudio.com/docs/configure/settings-sync)). Path-specific keys to consider excluding or converting to machine scope are `todo-tree.ripgrep.ripgrep`, `python-envs.pythonProjects`, and `python.defaultInterpreterPath`. VS Code's settings schema marks `settingsSync.ignoredSettings` itself as disallowed from the ignored list; its cross-device behavior should be tested before relying on it. Do not edit or sync any Windows file in this build.

## Growth Rules
| Growth source | Standing rule | One check command |
|---|---|---|
| VS Code server size and hashes | Keep only active server hashes; inventory before deletion and require no attached editor | `du -sh ~/.vscode-server; find ~/.vscode-server/bin -mindepth 1 -maxdepth 1 -type d -printf '%f %TY-%Tm-%Td\n'` |
| WSL extension count | Keep the reviewed shared WSL set; record count after install-loop changes | `code --list-extensions --show-versions | wc -l` |
| Worktrees and dependencies | One active session per branch; per-worktree installs; never delete worktrees/dependencies without approval | `git -C ~/projects/<repo> worktree list --porcelain; find ~/projects -xdev -type d \( -name node_modules -o -name .venv \) -prune -exec du -sh {} +` |
| Logs | Keep bounded logs and inspect age/size before cleanup | `find ~/.vscode-server/data/logs -type f -printf '%s %TY-%Tm-%Td %p\n' | sort -nr | head -30` |
| Tailscaled and sshd health | Keep Dell `tailscaled` active; install/enable keyed `sshd` on 2222 only if user reconfirms the fallback | `systemctl is-active tailscaled; systemctl is-active ssh ssh.socket 2>/dev/null; ss -ltn | rg ':(22|2222)\b'` |
| Tailscale key expiry | User confirmed expiry disabled for `dell-wsl`; preserve this state and recheck after node recreation | `tailscale status --json | jq -r '.Self | [.DNSName,.KeyExpiry] | @tsv'` |
| Repo ahead/behind drift | Fetch, report branch tracking and ahead/behind; do not auto-merge/push | `git -C ~/projects/<repo> fetch --quiet && git -C ~/projects/<repo> status --short --branch` |

## Exact `.wslconfig` Diff
Microsoft's current reference places `memory`, `processors`, `swap`, `swapFile`/example `swapfile`, `vmIdleTimeout`, `networkingMode`, and `firewall` under `[wsl2]`; `instanceIdleTimeout` under `[general]`; and `autoMemoryReclaim`/`sparseVhd` under `[experimental]`. The table uses camel-case `swapFile`, while Microsoft's example uses lowercase `swapfile`; the requested lowercase spelling was retained. Source: [Advanced settings configuration in WSL](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/wsl/wsl-config). The open-source WSL parser also contains both idle-timeout keys. WSL 2.4.13.0 was live before the update; the host script updates WSL, prints its resulting version, then rejects surfaced unknown/invalid-key errors rather than guessing.
```diff
 [wsl2]
 networkingMode=mirrored
 firewall=true
-memory=16GB
-processors=8
+memory=20GB
+processors=10
+swap=8GB
+swapfile=D:\\WSL\\swap.vhdx
+vmIdleTimeout=-1

+[general]
+instanceIdleTimeout=-1

+[experimental]
+autoMemoryReclaim=gradual
+sparseVhd=true
```
Applied file read-back passed every exact key. Backup: `C:\Users\Anant Gupta\.wslconfig.[REDACTED].bak`. Both idle keys are deliberate because the Dell is the always-on Build 2 SSH host.
`sparseVhd=true` makes newly-created VHDs sparse according to the current reference; it does not convert this existing Ubuntu VHDX. WSL 3.0.1's release notes mention a non-ASCII configuration-handling fix but do not discuss these individual keys or sparse conversion. The safe `wsl --manage Ubuntu --set-sparse true` request was refused on this machine with the explicit potential-data-corruption safeguard. `wsl.exe --help` lists `--set-sparse` and `--compact` under `--manage`, but not `--allow-unsafe`; `wsl.exe --manage --help` is invalid syntax. This proves behavior on this build/VHDX, not whether the refusal is build-specific or a standing policy. Never add the unsafe override; record safe refusal as SKIPPED and continue host verification.

| starship | Installed release binary `1.26.0`; config parse and Bash hook verified |
| win32yank | Installed x64 release `0.1.1`; PE/usage interface verified because the binary implements neither `--version` nor `--help` |
| gh-dash | Installed direct release `4.26.0` without a Git operation; `gh extension list` discovers `gh dash` |
| Chafa | Installed official static binary `1.18.3`, satisfying Yazi's >=1.16 requirement |
| Semgrep | Installed through uv tool at `1.179.0` |
| tmux / plugins | tmux `3.4`; TPM, resurrect, continuum, tmux-fzf, `samleeney/tmux-agent-status`, and `CRThaze/tmux-handlr` installed from archives; config parsed with `C-a` and pane labels |
| Antigravity `agy` | Official install is queued in `build1-user-step.sh` because the command guard rejected its temporary-file cleanup before launch; the sudo script runs it as the normal user and verifies it |
| fd / bat / ncdu / direnv | Queued in `build1-sudo-step.sh`; aliases and direnv activation occur only after version checks |
| wslu / wslview | Retest is in the post-update host script; failure enables the prepared PowerShell-backed `BROWSER` wrapper without patching system files |

Starship, Yazi, lazygit, Atuin, sesh, gh-dash, tmux, and the consolidated Bash block are explicitly labeled **“Build 1: reconstructed from Acer notes, not copied from Acer.”** No AI-platform home, SSH key, token, or `.mcp.env` was copied.
The ncdu config (`-e`, dark color, `.git`/`node_modules` excludes, confirm-quit) is also prepared and labeled from notes. Git's global `core.pager=delta` was not changed because all Git operations/config mutations are on hold; lazygit itself is wired to delta. gh-dash `repoPaths` remains empty until Build 2 deliberately maps live remotes without touching repository state.
## Decisions
- **Repair in place.** There is no ext4/systemd corruption, repos contain uncommitted and diverged work, and rebuilding would add data risk without fixing the scheduled-task/network lifecycle causes.
- **Keep mirrored networking.** It is noisy, but both Windows-host Obsidian MCP endpoints are live through it; NAT would break this known workflow.
- **Do not treat short WSL boots as random corruption.** The 30-minute cadence is explained by `ConversationCapture-Backfill-WSL`. Any change to that task belongs to the Windows-host session.
- **Do not force sparse conversion.** `sparseVhd=true` applies to newly created VHDs; if safe conversion of this existing VHDX is refused, record SKIPPED and continue independent health checks. Never use `--allow-unsafe`.
- **Do not copy AI homes or secrets.** Existing mode-600 `.mcp.env`, Codex auth, GitHub hosts, and mode-700 SSH directory were checked only for permissions; no contents were printed or copied.
- **Defer Git operations.** Local refs show real divergence and active work. No fetch/pull/rebase/reset/commit/push is part of Build 1 without explicit repo-by-repo approval.
- **Make this Dell an always-on SSH host.** Decision 3 requires both idle timers at `-1`; `autoMemoryReclaim=gradual` limits reclaimable cache pressure without silently stopping the instance.
- **Delete only manifest-matching artifacts.** Every removed path was rechecked for Git ignore status, an enclosing rebuild manifest, config/unit/cron references, open files/processes, and unchanged size. Zero paths failed the deletion-time gate.
- **Do not bypass Cargo's cache ownership.** Stable Cargo 1.99 has automatic age-based collection but no stable manual global-cache command; its 0.352 GiB registry/git cache stayed intact instead of being manually removed.
## Commands That Changed State
- `uv self update`; `rustup update`; `kiro-cli update --non-interactive`.
- Official/GitHub release downloads plus `install` placed delta, lazygit, Yazi/Ya, sesh, zoxide, Atuin, Starship, win32yank, Chafa, and gh-dash in user space; `uv tool install --force semgrep` installed Semgrep.
- Release archives populated `~/.tmux/plugins` and Yazi plugin/flavor directories without `git clone`.
- `apply_patch` created the notes-derived config files and consolidated Bash hooks; a short isolated tmux server verified the tmux config and was stopped.
- The approved artifact script removed the 82 paths in the manifest below with `rm -rf` only after the fresh gate, largest first.
- `npm cache clean --force`; `pnpm store prune`; `python3 -m pip cache purge`; `uv cache clean`.
- `apply_patch` created `wsl-browser`, `build1-user-step.sh`, `build1-sudo-step.sh`, `wsl-host-step.ps1`, and `wsl-idle-test.ps1`; Bash and PowerShell parsers passed.
- 2026-10-06 Windows host run updated WSL to 3.0.1.0 and shut WSL down as that script required; safe sparse conversion then refused. On 2026-10-07 `cp -p` backed up both scripts and `apply_patch` replaced them with ASCII PowerShell 5.1-compatible versions. The Windows PowerShell parser passed both. The final corrected host verifier passed start/config, memory, CPU, swap, VHDX status, sparse-skip, and `wslview`; it reported `systemd degraded` with one failed unit and measured 0 CheckConnection events over its 120-second window. A first test found bare `swapon` is not in WSL `--exec` PATH; the script now uses `/usr/sbin/swapon`. A first port-probe attempt had PowerShell/Bash quoting failure and was removed; separate WSL-side TCP checks passed both Obsidian endpoints. The corrected 75-second idle test passed from Windows after the user closed WSL/editor/Docker/Codex sessions: `wsl -l --runn
- 2026-10-07 post-update host-health check returned `systemd degraded`, with only `systemd-binfmt.service` failed; its journal details are no longer available. The current interop registration exists and wslview passes, but the failed unit remains unresolved. Kernel scan returned zero storage/I/O/OOM/panic matches. Added the same systemd check to the post-update script; parser and command passed, and the warning output was reproduced.
- `cp -p` created `.wslconfig.[REDACTED].bak`; `apply_patch` wrote the approved `.wslconfig`; immediate Bash read-back verified all 12 keys/section lines.
- Required Jarvis findings/index/session-log updates used `apply_patch`. No Git, Docker, editor-server, AI-home, C:-cleanup, or Windows task operation ran.

    if sec and sec.startswith("=== winget"):
        if ln.startswith("Name") or ln.startswith("---") or not ln.strip(): continue
        parts=re.split(r"\s{2,}",ln.strip())
        if len(parts)>=2:
            ident=parts[1]
            if ident.startswith("MSIX\\") and not re.search(r"Dell|Intel|AppUp|Malware|Logi|Teams|Todo|Sticky|Photos|Outlook|ChatGPT|Netflix|LinkedIn|HP|Canvas|CommandPalette|PowerAutomate|Cross|Weather|News|Bing|ZuneVideo|AIToolkit|Feedback|Mail|Paint|QuickAssist|Copilot|aimgr", ident): continue
            out.append(" | ".join(parts[:3]))
    else:
        out.append(ln[:170])
print("\n".join(out))
PY

# Manual, opt-in bootstrap for a new Windows-native Python project.
# Creates .venv\Scripts\python.exe, which VS Code's global
# python.defaultInterpreterPath already picks up automatically.
function New-PyEnv {
    param([switch]$Init)
    uv venv
    if ($Init) { uv init }
    Write-Host "Created .venv - reopen/reload this folder's VS Code window to activate it in new terminals." -ForegroundColor Green

## Old Laptop

`Old Laptop`: `40_Resources/CS/Concepts/New Laptop/Installations`. Everything above this heading describes the Acer (new laptop). This section is the installed-tool record for the Dell Latitude 5530, written 2026-10-11 from live queries on the machine and reconciled with [[Old Laptop Rebuild - Build 1 WSL Findings]], [[Old Laptop Rebuild - Build 2 VS Code and Two-Laptop Findings]] and [[Old Laptop Rebuild - Build 3 Windows Cleanup Findings]]. Where a build note and the machine disagreed, the machine is shown.

Machine: i7-1255U (10 cores, 12 threads), 32 GB RAM, Intel Iris Xe only (no NVIDIA GPU), one 1 TB NVMe split into C: (252 GB) and D: (700 GB). Windows 11 Pro build 26300, user `C:\Users\Anant Gupta`. WSL 3.0.1.0, kernel 6.18.40.1, distro `Ubuntu` (Ubuntu 24.04.4), Linux user `anant_gupta`, mirrored networking, swap on `D:\WSL\swap.vhdx`. This is the always-on SSH host for the Acer: Tailscale runs inside this WSL only and is never installed on this laptop's Windows side.

### Where things stand
- WSL is rebuilt and configured (Builds 1 and 2). Its terminal configs were reconstructed from notes and mostly differ from the Acer's real files (see "Config parity" below).
- The Windows side is a pre-rebuild state: no PowerShell 7, no Starship, no Nerd Font, Windows Terminal on defaults, and a PowerShell profile that only loads opam, Kiro shell integration and a `New-PyEnv` helper. Build 3 (cleanup) has audited it and has not changed anything yet. Build 4 installs and configures the terminal toolkit.
- Several C: items are pending removal or review in Build 3 (Microsoft 365, Malwarebytes, Respondus, MongoDB Shell, WinRAR, Dell Optimizer and SupportAssist plugin, stray executables). They are listed here as installed because they still are.

### Config parity with the Acer snapshot (2026-10-11)
The Acer's real WSL and Windows files are in `Old Laptop Rebuild/Acer Dotfile Snapshot/`. Compared by SHA-256 with the Dell's WSL files: **4 of 17 comparable files match** (the four Yazi plugin Lua files). **13 differ**: atuin config, gh-dash config, git ignore, lazygit config, ncdu config, sesh config, `starship.toml`, Yazi `init.lua`, `keymap.toml`, `theme.toml`, `yazi.toml`, `.gitconfig` and `.tmux.conf`. These Dell files were reconstructed from notes in Build 1 and carry a "from notes, not from Acer" label. Three Yazi files are missing on the Dell (`flavors/tokyonight-storm.yazi/flavor.toml` and `tmtheme.xml`, and `package.toml`). The Dell's `.wslconfig` differs from the Acer's on purpose (20 GB, 10 CPUs, swap on D:, both idle timeouts disabled for the SSH host). The Acer's own Windows and WSL `starship.toml` files are identical except line endings (the WSL copy has CRLF); the LF version is the one to keep.

### Windows terminal on the Acer (from its snapshot)
The Acer's Windows side is also incomplete: Windows Terminal is on defaults (One Half Dark, `IosevkaTerm Nerd Font`, 85 percent acrylic, default profile Windows PowerShell), no PowerShell profile exists, the global git config has only `longpaths`, `fsmonitor` and `untrackedCache` (plus redacted identity), and Yazi, lazygit, bat, delta and gh-dash exist only inside its WSL. Its winget export includes PowerShell 7, Starship, Tailscale, NanaZip, ripgrep, jq, Yazi (installed, unconfigured), Node LTS, Docker Desktop, Ollama, VS Code, Cursor, Claude and Antigravity. Build 4 completes this side on both laptops.

### How to refresh this section
Run on the Dell and re-read: `winget list`, `choco list`, `npm ls -g --depth=0` (Windows and WSL), `uv tool list`, `cargo install --list`, `code --list-extensions --show-versions` (Windows and WSL), `apt-mark showmanual`, and `ls ~/.local/bin`. The inventory scripts used for this section were `wsl-inventory.sh` and `win-inventory.ps1`; Build 4 turns them into repeatable scripts.

import io, sys
path, sec = sys.argv[1], sys.argv[2]
text = io.open(path, encoding="utf-8", newline="").read()
add = io.open(sec, encoding="utf-8").read()
marker = "\n## Flashcards"
assert text.count(marker) == 1, "flashcards marker count"
assert "\n## Old Laptop\n" not in text, "section already present"
i = text.index(marker)
text = text[:i].rstrip("\n") + "\n\n" + add.rstrip("\n") + "\n" + text[i:]
text = text.replace("updated: 2026-09-19", "updated: 2026-10-11", 1)
io.open(path, "w", encoding="utf-8", newline="").write(text)
print("inserted")

$ErrorActionPreference = 'Stop'

$Snap = "D:\_Anant\20_Progress\Documents\Jarvis\40_Resources\CS\Concepts\New Laptop\Old Laptop Rebuild\Acer Dotfile Snapshot"
$Win  = Join-Path $Snap "windows"
$Wsl  = Join-Path $Snap "wsl"
$H    = $env:USERPROFILE

New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force -Path $Snap | Out-Null
New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force -Path $Win  | Out-Null
New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force -Path $Wsl  | Out-Null

# --- secret patterns (heuristic) ---
$SecretPatterns = @(
  'AKIA[0-9A-Z]{16}',
  'gh[pousr]_[A-Za-z0-9]{30,}',
  'github_pat_[A-Za-z0-9_]{30,}',
  'sk-[A-Za-z0-9]{20,}',
  'xox[baprs]-[A-Za-z0-9-]{10,}',
  '-----BEGIN[ A-Z]*PRIVATE KEY-----',
  'AIza[0-9A-Za-z\-_]{35}',
  'ya29\.[0-9A-Za-z\-_]+',
  '(?i)\b(api[_-]?key|secret|passwd|password)\b\s*[=:]\s*[''"]?[A-Za-z0-9/_\-\.]{8,}',
  '(?i)Bearer\s+[A-Za-z0-9\-_\.]{15,}'
)

# --- forbidden source paths (AI-tool / credential directories - defense in depth) ---
$ForbiddenMarkers = @('.claude', '.codex', '.cursor', '.kiro', '.ssh', '.mcp.json', '.mcp.env')

function Test-SecretText {
  param([string]$Text)
  foreach ($pat in $SecretPatterns) {
    if ($Text -match $pat) { return $true }
  }
  return $false
}

function Test-SecretFile {
  param([string]$Path)
  try {
    $content = Get-Content -Raw -Path $Path -ErrorAction Stop
  } catch { return $false }
  if ($null -eq $content) { return $false }
  return Test-SecretText $content
}

function Test-ForbiddenPath {
  param([string]$Path)
  foreach ($m in $ForbiddenMarkers) {
    if ($Path -like "*$m*") { return $true }
  }
  return $false
}

# hashes-windows.txt columns: RelDest  Source  SourceSHA256  CopySHA256  CopySize  Status
$HashLines = New-Object System.Collections.Generic.List[string]
$HashLines.Add("RelDest`tSource`tSourceSHA256`tCopySHA256`tCopySize`tStatus")
# excluded-windows.txt columns: Source  RelDest  Reason
$ExcludedLines = New-Object System.Collections.Generic.List[string]
$ExcludedLines.Add("Source`tRelDest`tReason")

function Add-Excluded {
  param([string]$Source, [string]$RelDest, [string]$Reason)
  $ExcludedLines.Add("$Source`t$RelDest`t$Reason")
}

function Add-Hash {
  param([string]$RelDest, [string]$Source, [string]$SourceHash, [string]$CopyHash, [long]$CopySize, [string]$Status)
  $HashLines.Add("$RelDest`t$Source`t$SourceHash`t$CopyHash`t$CopySize`t$Status")
}

# Plain, verbatim, binary-safe copy. Scans the SOURCE before ever writing a copy.
function Export-File {
  param([string]$Source, [string]$RelDest)
  if (Test-ForbiddenPath $Source) {
    Add-Excluded $Source $RelDest "AI-tool or credential directory - excluded by policy"
    return
  }
  if (-not (Test-Path $Source -PathType Leaf)) {
    Add-Excluded $Source $RelDest "not present on this host"
    return
  }
  if (Test-SecretFile $Source) {
    Add-Excluded $Source $RelDest "matched secret heuristic - not copied"
    return
  }
  $destPath = Join-Path $Win $RelDest
  New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force -Path (Split-Path $destPath -Parent) | Out-Null
  Copy-Item -Path $Source -Destination $destPath -Force
  $srcHash = (Get-FileHash -Algorithm SHA256 -Path $Source).Hash
  $dstHash = (Get-FileHash -Algorithm SHA256 -Path $destPath).Hash
  ```

<design>
Apply every rule below to every script, not only the first one.
- One manifest drives everything. Create Old Laptop Rebuild\Windows Toolkit\toolkit.json. Each tool has: id, display name, category, winget id or other install method, version pin (optional), machines (both, dell or acer), install-location policy, verify command, expected-version pattern, config files it owns, and use-case fields (purpose, when to reach for it, key commands, where it runs: Windows, WSL or both, notes for an AI agent). The install script, the verify script and the catalog generator all read this one file.
- Scripts live in Old Laptop Rebuild\Windows Toolkit\_scripts\ and are numbered: 00-preflight.ps1 (read-only checks and the plan), 10-install-toolkit.ps1, 20-apply-configs.ps1, 30-wsl-config-parity.sh (the Dell's WSL), 40-build-catalog.ps1, 99-verify.ps1, plus a README with the run order and rollback. Add scripts only when a step cannot live in one of these.
- Every script is ASCII only, can be run twice with the same result, supports a -DryRun switch that prints what it would change, detects the machine (COMPUTERNAME and user path) and the vault root, writes a log under D:\WSL\ops\windows\logs\ and a result JSON into the vault under Windows Toolkit\results\<host>\, never prints secret values, never deletes anything except replacing a config file after a timestamped backup, and exits non-zero when a required step fails. Installs continue past one failed package and print a summary table.
- Installs use winget only: --exact, --accept-package-agreements, --accept-source-agreements, --disable-interactivity, and --location D:\Apps\<Name> only when the installer supports it. Portable CLI tools and fonts land under the user profile on C: and are small, so record each tool's size and drive. Machine-wide installers such as PowerShell 7 raise a UAC prompt; the script says so before it starts.
- Versions: the Dell run records the installed versions into Windows Toolkit\toolkit.lock.json. The Acer run installs those versions where winget has them, so both laptops end up on the same versions.
- Config files are written once into Windows Toolkit\configs\ as UTF-8 without BOM and LF line endings, using [IO.File]::WriteAllText (Set-Content in Windows PowerShell adds CRLF and a BOM). The apply script copies them to each machine, points tools at the shared location with user environment variables where the tool looks elsewhere on Windows (STARSHIP_CONFIG, YAZI_CONFIG_HOME and the equivalents; check each tool's current documentation), and writes a SHA-256 table. A config is "identical" only when its hash matches the source.
- Machine differences are explicit blocks guarded by machine or tool checks, never copies of the file with edits. The Dell's PowerShell profile keeps its opam, Kiro and New-PyEnv blocks; the Acer's does not need them. Tailscale is in the manifest for the Acer only. Do not touch Tailscale on either machine.
- Checks that mean something: parse every script with [System.Management.Automation.Language.Parser]::ParseFile, run each in -DryRun on the Dell, and let 99-verify.ps1 compare manifest against reality (installed version matches pattern, config hash matches source, env vars set, font family registered, profile loads in a new shell, Starship renders). A script that only started without error is not a check.
</design>

<toolkit_candidates>

- Shell and terminal, both laptops: PowerShell 7, Windows Terminal (settings below), Starship, an IosevkaTerm Nerd Font Mono package that registers as "IosevkaTerm NFM" (find the right winget package or release, verify its checksum), zoxide, fzf, fd, bat.
- Core CLI, both: Git (current), delta, GitHub CLI, ripgrep, jq, FFmpeg, NanaZip (the Acer has it; use one archive tool on both).
- Runtimes and package managers, both: Node LTS with Corepack and pnpm, uv by its standalone installer so it comes first on PATH, Python through uv, bun if an installed AI tool needs it. Decide with me: Node 22 vs LTS (the Dell has 22, the Acer has LTS), the pip-installed uv on the Dell, and where Miniconda belongs (the plan says D:\conda). Do not change a runtime manager without my approval.
- AI platforms and agents, both: verify Claude Code (native), Codex CLI (npm, pinned), Claude Desktop, Cursor, Kiro, Antigravity, Docker Desktop and Ollama; add Windows CLIs for the others only when they exist and the Acer uses them.
- MCP prerequisites, both: npx (Node), uvx (uv), mcp-remote, Docker for container-based servers. Check, by name only, that the launch command of each configured MCP server in Claude Desktop, Claude Code user scope and Codex resolves on this machine.
- Per machine: Tailscale on the Acer's Windows side only (verify, never install on the Dell).
- Optional tier, WSL-first, not on Windows unless I approve: Yazi, lazygit, tmux, atuin, sesh, ncdu.
</toolkit_candidates>

- Windows Terminal settings.json: PowerShell 7 as the default profile; the font family IosevkaTerm Nerd Font Mono; a Tokyo Night Storm color scheme to match Starship, Yazi and tmux (the Acer currently uses One Half Dark; I decide); the Acer's other settings (opacity, acrylic, cursor, keybindings) kept unless I say otherwise; the Ubuntu profile named per machine; Miniconda profiles left to Miniconda.

- Environment variables at user scope: STARSHIP_CONFIG and the config-location variables; leave the cache variables to Build 3 and only verify them.

Compare the Acer's .bashrc block and tmux plugin list against the Dell's and report the differences instead of overwriting them.

<task>
Work in these phases, in order. Finish and check each phase before starting the next. Keep working until everything I asked for is done, and stop to ask only when you cannot go on without me or before a risky step. When the work is done and checked, stop and report. Do not add tools, features or files that I did not ask for; mention a good idea at the end instead.
Phase 1, audit (read-only). Read everything in the reading list. Re-check the Dell's Windows and WSL state live. Build current-state tables for the Dell (live) and the Acer (from the snapshot), a parity table for the WSL config files, and note what Build 3 has already changed.
Phase 2, design checkpoint. Write the design to the vault, then stop and show me: the final toolkit manifest as a table (tool, why it is there, machines, install method); every decision I need to make, each with your recommendation (Node 22 or LTS, uv standalone, Miniconda, git autocrlf, Windows Terminal scheme and font, profile contents, which WSL configs get overwritten, whether to keep Ollama and PowerShell 7 inside the Dell's WSL, which missing binaries such as ruff, shfmt and shellcheck to install); the script list with the order I will run them; the catalog outline; and the Acer plan. Wait for my reply.
Phase 3, write. Write the scripts, manifest and configs to the vault. Parse-check every script and run each one in -DryRun on the Dell. Give me the Dell run order as copy-ready commands, one per line, each with the quoted full path and the PowerShell call that runs it as a file, and tell me which ones need an elevated PowerShell and which can run as me. Then wait.
Phase 4, Dell run. I run the scripts one at a time and tell you when each has run. After each, read its log and result JSON, fix problems in the script, and give me the next command. Give me the individual commands I should run by hand (UAC installers, anything interactive) as separate lines. Repeat until 99-verify.ps1 passes, then write toolkit.lock.json.
Phase 5, catalog and agent guide. Run 40-build-catalog.ps1 so it writes Windows Toolkit\catalog\Tool Catalog - <host>.md and tool-catalog-<host>.json (every tool on Windows and in WSL with version, path, drive, owner config file and manifest use-case fields). Then write, from official documentation and my notes, one authored note next to Installations: "Machine Toolkit - Tool Use Cases and Agent Guide". It has an agent front page (which shell for what, PowerShell or WSL; where projects live and why never under /mnt; where the vault is; which AI platform is for what; which MCP servers exist, by name and purpose; the sync boundaries between Git, Syncthing and Google Drive; secrets rules), then one card per tool: what it is, when to reach for it, three to six copyable commands, its config path, gotchas learned in these builds, and a one-line instruction I can give an agent. Group the cards by task (find files, search code, git work, AI agents, MCP, containers, models, networking, terminal navigation). Propose, but do not write, a pointer line for the Windows home AGENTS.md.
Phase 6, Acer. Write "Acer Run Sheet" in Windows Toolkit\: the exact copy-ready commands for the Acer in order (the scripts are already in the vault, so the sheet only says where they are on the Acer and how the vault path differs), what each prints, what differs from the Dell and why, and a reminder that Tailscale is already on the Acer's Windows side and must not be added to its WSL. I run them on the Acer. When I tell you they ran, read the result JSON from both machines and write the parity matrix: every tool and config, columns Dell and Acer, versions and hashes, PASS or DIFF, a reason for each DIFF.
Phase 7, close. Update the "Old Laptop" section of Installations to match the final state, add an "Acer, Windows side" section next to it, add Growth rules (the standing rule and one check command for tool versions, config hashes, font registration, environment variables, startup time and catalog freshness), write the findings note, and update the Index.
</task>

# Old Laptop Rebuild - Windows Prompts (Build 3 C Drive Cleanup and Build 4 Terminal Parity)

Two prompts for Claude Code sessions on the Dell's Windows side, written against the Sonnet 5.5 prompting guide (task, intent and constraints in the first turn; literal scoping; reasons next to rules; the "keep working" and "stop and report" paragraphs; a real

## How to run
- **Where:** Claude Code in PowerShell on the Dell, started from `C:\Users\Anant Gupta`. Pick Sonnet 5.5 with `/model`.
- **Effort:** `high` for both. Build 3 involves judgment, images and deletions. Build 4 designs a manifest, writes and debugs several scripts and authors a catalog.
- **Order:** Build 3's audit is done, so run its approved phases first. Build 4's Phases 1 and 2 are read-only and can start earlier, but run its install scripts after Build 3's Tier A cleanup so C: has room. In Build 4 the session writes scripts and you run t
- **Images:** paste the seven WizTree screenshots (C: three, D: four) in the same message as the Build 3 prompt. Build 4 gets none.
- **Admin steps:** the sessions run without elevation and write every admin step to a script under `D:\WSL\ops\windows\` for you to run in an elevated PowerShell.
- **Numbering:** Build 1 was WSL, Build 2 was WSL VS Code plus the SSH workflow. These are Build 3 and Build 4. Windows VS Code and MCP wiring become Build 5, and the dev-only profile with the final check against the Acer becomes Build 6.

Below are my answers to your open decisions. Execute Phase 3 now, one numbered step at a time, verifying each step before starting the next. Re-read your own Build 1 WSL Findings note first, and read the "Decision 3" section of Codebases/Codebases - Two-Laptop SSH Workflow - Locked Decisions and Postmortem (same vault folder tree, 40_Resources/CS/Concepts/New Laptop/). The final file must contain, under [wsl2]: networkingMode=mirrored, firewall=true, memory=20GB, processors=10, swap=8GB, swapfile=D:\\WSL\\swap.vhdx, vmIdleTimeout=-1. Under [general]: instanceIdleTimeout=-1. Under [experimental]: autoMemoryReclaim=gradual, sparseVhd=true. Confirm each key name and section against the current Microsoft WSL configuration reference and against the WSL version that exists after `wsl --update`. If the updated WSL does not recognize a key, tell me instead of guessing. Both idle keys are required together: vmIdleTimeout alone does not keep the distro alive past the 15-second instanceIdleTimeout. This means the VM never auto-shuts down. That is deliberate, and autoMemoryReclaim keeps memory in check. 2. Cache cleanup: npm, pnpm store, pip, cargo, uv, using each tool's own cleanup command. Record sizes before and after. 3. Toolchain parity: install everything on your "missing or incomplete" list in user space, plus Antigravity (agy), and update uv, rustup and Kiro CLI if their installers support it. Prefer GitHub release binaries over `cargo install` (delta, lazygit, yazi, sesh, zoxide, atuin, starship, win32yank), because compiling creates new cache. Verify each tool with its own --version right after installing it. Build configs "from notes, not from Acer" and label them. Add shell hooks only after the binary is verified. fd and bat need the `fdfind` and `batcat` symlinks in ~/.local/bin. For wslu: re-test after the WSL update. The test is valid only when no other WSL terminal, VS Code window or Codex session is open, so print that precondition first. A match means the file is excluded and logged by path and reason only. - It redacts the [credential ...] and [user] sections from the .gitconfig copies by key name. - It never prints a secret value. - Copies keep the original bytes (a binary-safe copy, no re-encoding). - It computes SHA-256 of each source file and of its copy, and writes both to hashes-windows.txt and hashes-wsl.txt in the snapshot folder with MATCH or DIFF per file. A DIFF is expected only for the redacted .gitconfig copies. - It leaves out everything under ~/.claude, ~/.codex, ~/.cursor, ~/.kiro, ~/.ssh, ~/.mcp.json, ~/.mcp.env and any other AI-tool or credential directory. - Yazi: only the toml files and flavor or plugin source, no caches. tmux: plugin names only. - It is ASCII only, can be run twice with the same result, and runs as a file. 2. Create Old Laptop Rebuild\Windows Toolkit\toolkit.json. Each tool has: id, display name, category, winget id or other install method, version pin (optional), machines (both, dell or acer), install-location policy, verify command, expected-version pattern, config files it owns, and use-case fields (purpose, when to reach for it, key commands, where it runs: Windows, WSL or both, notes for an AI agent). The install script, the verify script and the catalog generator all read this one file. - Scripts live in Old Laptop Rebuild\Windows Toolkit\_scripts\ and are numbered: 00-preflight.ps1 (read-only checks and the plan), 10-install-toolkit.ps1, 20-apply-configs.ps1, 30-wsl-config-parity.sh (the Dell's WSL), 40-build-catalog.ps1, 99-verify.ps1, plus a README with the run order and rollback. Add scripts only when a step cannot live in one of these. - Every script is ASCII only, can be run twice with the same result, supports a -DryRun switch that prints what it would change, detects the machine (COMPUTERNAME and user path) and the vault root, writes a log under D:\WSL\ops\windows\logs\ and a result JSON into the vault under Windows Toolkit\results\<host>\, never prints secret values, never deletes anything except replacing a config file after a timestamped backup, and exits non-zero when a required step fails. Installs continue past one failed package and print a summary table. - Installs use winget only: --exact, --accept-package-agreements, --accept-source-agreements, --disable-interactivity, and --location D:\Apps\<Name> only when the installer supports it. Portable CLI tools and fonts land under the user profile on C: and are small, so record each tool's size and drive. Machine-wide installers such as PowerShell 7 raise a UAC prompt; the script says so before it starts. - Versions: the Dell run records the installed versions into Windows Toolkit\toolkit.lock.json. The Acer run installs those versions where winget has them, so both laptops end up on the same versions. - Config files are written once into Windows Toolkit\configs\ as UTF-8 without BOM and LF line endings, using [IO.File]::WriteAllText (Set-Content in Windows PowerShell adds CRLF and a BOM). The apply script copies them to each machine, points tools at the shared location with user environment variables where the tool looks elsewhere on Windows (STARSHIP_CONFIG, YAZI_CONFIG_HOME and the equivalents; check each tool's current documentation), and writes a SHA-256 table. A config is "identical" only when its hash matches the source. - Machine differences are explicit blocks guarded by machine or tool checks, never copies of the file with edits. The Dell's PowerShell profile keeps its opam, Kiro and New-PyEnv blocks; the Acer's does not need them. Tailscale is in the manifest for the Acer only. Do not touch Tailscale on either machine. - Checks that mean something: parse every script with [System.Management.Automation.Language.Parser]::ParseFile, run each in -DryRun on the Dell, and let 99-verify.ps1 compare manifest against reality (installed version matches pattern, config hash matches source, env vars set, font family registered, profile loads in a new shell, Starship renders). A script that only started without error is not a check. </design> <toolkit_candidates> Start the manifest from these candidates.
